#6930. This Charming 1960’s Alabama Cottage Unites Classic Southern Style With Family Heirlooms

There’s room for everyone, grandchildren included.

The best houses keep family close, whether that means an expansive living room that fits all the siblings and their spouses, room for another generation under the same roof, or grandparents just a few minutes down the road. For Leigh and Kevin Misso, founders of River Brook Design & Construction, that sentiment became the starting point for a deeply personal project: transforming a dated 1960s house in Mountain Brook, Alabama, into a new home for Leigh's mother and stepfather, who moved to be closer to their grandchildren.

At 2,600 square feet, the three-bedroom house wasn’t particularly grand, but that was precisely its appeal. “We wanted to embrace its modest scale rather than fight against it,” says Leigh. The couple undertook a complete renovation, adding square footage, vaulting ceilings, and reimagining the once-cramped kitchen as an airy gathering place. Throughout, the goal was to preserve the home’s cottage-like charm while giving it the breathing room to accommodate a growing family. “Every detail was thoughtfully considered to make this a place where children and grandchildren feel at home, generations can gather, and meaningful family traditions and memories can be made for years to come,” adds Leigh.

The resulting interiors are crisp and refined, while still holding decades of family history. Leigh’s grandmother's antique sideboard anchors the dining room, while her wicker sofa finds new life on the sunroom porch, dressed in blue-and-white Les Touches fabric. A four-poster bed passed down through her stepfather’s family takes pride of place in the primary bedroom. Elsewhere, mustard velvet, richly colored built-ins, and playful painted stripes add a fresh perspective to traditional Southern decorating.

Of course, the house’s greatest luxury might be its expansive rear porch, where a custom hanging daybed beckons, and where Leigh’s mother spends much of her time. It was one of the renovation’s biggest investments, but for a family whose entire project was predicated on spending more time together, the payoff was worth it. “Ultimately, our goal was to create a home that feels just as beautiful and comfortable for quiet everyday moments as it does when the entire family comes together,” says Leah.

FAST FACTSDesigner: River Brook Design & ConstructionLocation: Mountain Brook, AlabamaThe Space: A three-bedroom, two-and-a-half-bathroom home across 2,600 square feet.

“This is one of my favorite little rooms in the house,” says Leigh of the family room, which doubles as a play space. “We wanted to create a cozy, intimate retreat that felt full of personality, warmth, and charm. I love that we embraced the smaller scale of the space rather than trying to make it feel larger. The result is a room that feels incredibly inviting, layered, and collected.”

Vaulted ceilings and an exposed wood beam give this modestly sized living room an impressive sense of scale. Below, Leigh and Kevin balanced a tailored Coley Home sofa with dark upholstered seating, antique armchairs, and a Louis Philippe mirror.

“Ultimately, we wanted this room to embody the charm of the entire home—small in scale but never small in feeling,” says Leigh. “It’s a space that feels elegant enough for entertaining yet relaxed enough for grandchildren to curl up on the sofa. That balance of beauty, comfort, and livability is always at the heart of our designs.”

Proof that the smallest corners can have the biggest personalities, this charming game nook was designed with the grandchildren in mind. “I especially love the juxtaposition of the modern white pedestal table with the rich mustard velvet chairs,” adds Leigh. “The sculptural shapes and playful color bring a fresh energy to the space, while the gallery wall of collected artwork adds history, personality, and a sense of nostalgia.”

The sunroom acts a bit like a family scrapbook, compiling a slew of heirloom and inherited pieces into a comfortable place to unwind. “We wanted this space to feel as though it had been collected over generations rather than decorated all at once. It's comfortable, timeless, and a little whimsical—the perfect place for grandchildren to gather, family to visit, and everyday memories to be made.”

“I love how this dining room feels intimate, layered, and full of personality. We wanted to create a space that felt a little more formal and collected, while still being warm and inviting for everyday family gatherings,” says Leigh of the gathering space, which includes an antique sideboard that used to belong to her grandmother. “The mix of family heirlooms, traditional patterns, and unexpected pops of color gives the room a sense of history without feeling overly serious.”

A far cry from the home’s original 1960s kitchen, this reimagined space pairs vaulted ceilings with traditional, furniture-inspired details. A built-in china cabinet lends a sense of old-world formality, while marble countertops, warm wood floors, and oversized pendants keep the room feeling polished. Behind the scenes, a concealed coffee bar and appliance cabinet ensure everyday necessities don't interrupt the view.

Sleepovers get their own stylish headquarters in the grandchildren's bunk room, where the team channeled a playful and imaginative attitude. “One of my favorite details is the painted stripe treatment that wraps the walls and ceiling,” says Leigh. “Rather than using wallpaper, we chose to paint the stripes, creating a custom look that adds depth, dimension, and a wonderful sense of enclosure.”

The heart of the primary bedroom is an eye-catching antique four-poster bed, which has been pass down through several generations of Leigh’s family. “It’s such a special piece, and I love that it brings a genuine sense of history and heritage to the space,” she adds. “Rather than designing the room around something new, we embraced this treasured family heirloom and allowed it to become the focal point.”

Leigh and Kevin Misso are the husband-and-wife founders of River Brook, a Birmingham, Alabama–based design-build studio specializing in thoughtfully crafted homes that balance timeless architecture with modern livability. Together, the duo brings a refined yet approachable perspective to residential design, emphasizing traditional details, layered interiors, and spaces that feel collected rather than decorated.

#6929. The Biggest Home Trends Buyers Will Want in 2027, According to New Zillow Data

Buyers want homes that feel polished, personal, and efficient.

The new year is on the horizon, and if you’re hoping to have a home that’s on trend for 2027, Zillow’s annual home trends report is a must-read. In the newly released study, the real estate marketplace analyzed the language in millions of for-sale listings to highlight the features most likely to drive the market.

In the year ahead, all signs point to a demand for a more polished aesthetic as Americans look to bring bold color and less tech into their homes.

“Digital detox is the new luxury,” says Amanda Pendleton, Zillow’s home trends expert. “Homes are getting smarter, but buyers also want tech-free zones where nothing needs charging. Taken together, this data signals that buyers value homes that are functional and efficient, but still feel polished and personal.”

Read on to discover the home trends setting the scene for 2027.

The word “cozy” may be among the most common adjectives in listings, but in the new year, folks are after a more refined look. Mentions for “elevated” homes are up by 71 percent, but buyers aren’t necessarily seeking the classic midcentury modern aesthetic. The emerging design scene favors bold expressions of painted brick, board-and-batten siding, and color-drenched interiors, all refined features that make a personal statement.

As the culture becomes increasingly entrenched in all things AI, some homebuyers are going all-in with tech immersion while others are begging to unplug. Mentions of AI are up a staggering 119 percent in listings, but homes that boast “digital detox” spaces are 34 percent more common than last year.

The market shows a decrease in the demand for sustainability features and a greater focus on home efficiency as “green” terms fall 26 percent. While you won’t see these details in listing photos, buyers are enticed by mentions of whole-home batteries, which have risen by 43 percent, water filtration systems, up by 22 percent, and radon mitigation, up by 18 percent.

While homebuyers haven’t completely abandoned the open floor plan, data shows a greater interest in defined spaces within the home as the “sandwich generation” revives the normalcy of multigenerational living. Interestingly, kitchens are not as enticing to buyers as home offices (up by 42 percent), media rooms (up by 37 percent), and nurseries (up by 9 percent) begin to demand greater focus.

This year saw an increased interest in at-home wellness features, and in 2027, the demand is only growing. Mentions of home gyms are up 18 percent, spa-inspired bathrooms have risen to 60 percent, and golf simulators are up 29 percent. Jacuzzis are fading from the design scene, replaced by cold plunge pools, with mentions rising by 44 percent.

Dogs may be man’s best friend, but cats are emerging as America’s pet, as mentions of dog parks are flat and dog-grooming stations have fallen 16 percent. Meanwhile, catios, which are screened-in outdoor spaces for cats, are appearing 32 percent more in for-sale listings.

#6926. Marshals Is Ruining the Dutton Family Legacy

The Yellowstone spinoff wants to move on from its Kevin Costner-sized shadow, but it can’t.

Ever since John Dutton (Kevin Costner) died, the Yellowstone-verse has sought to do away with everything he stood for. Without the patriarch in charge, it’s dawned on his children, Kayce (Luke Grimes) and Beth (Kelly Reilly), that the man was too violent and too stubborn. So, Kayce sold the ranch and resolved to put his violent life behind in the spinoff, Marshals, while Beth moved to Texas in Dutton Ranch to try ranching her own way.

It’s not really working out. Beth and her husband Rip (Cole Hauser) basically just moved the whole Dutton operation to Texas. And as much as Kayce’s plan to turn his last remaining acres of the Yellowstone Ranch into an equine therapy center would follow through on his plan for peace, he’s too busy continuing his father’s cycle of violence by running into danger as a member of a team of U.S. Marshals every week. Why? Well, because peace for the Duttons just isn’t good TV.

Still, Marshals tries its best to weave the framework into season 2. The latest round of episodes to kick off the second season deals heavily with post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD), and the various ways the Marshals team handles their crazy, violent lives. Mostly, it’s by bottling it up and never speaking about it. After killing a man in the season 1 finale, Kayce’s son Tate (Brecken Merrill) says a line in season 2, episode 3 that could have been plucked directly from a Dutton family quote book: “What happened stays where it is.”

But try as they might, the Dutton children are becoming their father anyway. By now in the task force spinoff, Kayce’s likely killed more men on the ranch than his father ever did. And though he’s not branding people like cattle anymore—which still haunts Kayce’s dreams in season 2, episode 4—even John Dutton gave up that practice after Yellowstone season 3.

Most likely, the Marshals team just wrote themselves into a trap. They might think they’re providing Kayce good backstory by tackling his inner quest for a peaceful life, but when he heads to work to kill criminals every morning, you can’t really believe the guy. Is he trying at all? And since he’s clearly not, maybe we should focus on that instead...?

So, at the end of episode 3, Kayce demands that Tate break his family’s generational cycle and take his ass to therapy. “It’s the last place my father would’ve taken me, and the last place I would’ve gone myself,” he says.

Two episodes have passed since that moment, and we haven’t seen Tate actually go yet. Plus, Kayce hasn’t exactly set a good example. Despite ordering his son to open up, Kayce didn’t give us any hints that he’s decided to see a therapist himself. And that equine center? We’ll be lucky if we ever see it until Marshals is over for good.

#6925. I Asked Designers for Their Secrets to an Expensive-Looking Guest Bedroom. They All Said the Same Thing

Skip the fussy pillow pile and focus on the details guests will actually notice.

Pictured above: a bold aubergine double guest bedroom designed by Nicole Fisher.

The holiday hosting season is upon us, and while you’re planning your tablescape decor and seasonal trimmings, you might want to take a hard look at your guest bedroom.

“Most guest rooms get treated like overflow storage, and guests feel that the second they walk in,” says Marie Cloud, principal designer at Indigo Pruitt.

Even if you host only occasionally, your guest room should give visitors a comfortable place to unwind. No need for a guest book or fuzzy slippers: Designers say these four thoughtful touches can make the room feel luxe without costing a fortune.

Regardless of the design aesthetic, the ambiance of a room always hinges on lighting. “I always pay attention to the lighting,” says Louis Lin, founder of his eponymous New York City–based interior design studio. “A beautiful bedside lamp with a warm, flattering glow can transform the atmosphere of a room. Layering different light sources, rather than relying on overhead lighting, can do that. It's a relatively simple gesture that makes a room feel much more intimate and refined.” This also gives guests the freedom to tailor the air of their space to their liking. It’s one of the few details they can truly control in their temporary lodging.

While a pile of pillows might look inviting, designer Marie Cloud says the bedtime legwork promptly cancels out the aesthetics.

“Skip the stack of eight decorative pillows your guest has to relocate before bed,” says Cloud. Her perfect formula for a top-notch guest setup? Two sleeping pillows, plus one lumbar pillow, all set atop a good set of sheets.

“Sheets with real hand-feel, like a crisp percale or washed linen,” Cloud suggests. “Finish with a throw folded at the foot in a color pulled from the room. It looks considered, and it’s actually comfortable at 2 a.m.”

Most guest rooms get the all-white wall treatment in the name of neutrality, but all it really says is that your space lacks intention. Just because this room is (mostly) reserved for guests doesn't mean it has to lack character. In fact, Cloud says you should not hold back.

“Drench the room,” says the designer. “Walls, trim, and ceiling in the same color, ideally something deep and warm. Continuous color reads custom, it makes a small room feel wrapped rather than cramped, and it tells your guest the space was designed for them. A guest room is also the lowest-risk room in the house to be bold, so take the swing.”

The best guest setups make visitors feel anticipated, even when they show up unexpectedly. To achieve the boutique-hotel feel, designers recommend investing in small luxuries that leave a big impact. A few items Cloud and Lin always recommend having on hand are a small luggage rack, real hangers, fresh towels, and a select set of toiletries reserved especially for guests.

“The real luxury is making guests feel that the room has been prepared just for them,” says Lin.

#6923. Three Clues That Suggest Callum Turner Could Already Be the Next James Bond

The final screen tests are seemingly finished, but we may already have our answer.

It's been over five years since the release of No Time to Die, Daniel Craig's final outing as James Bond, and the speculation surrounding his successor has hit a fever pitch.

Recent reports suggested that the final screen tests have already happened. It's widely thought that Jack Lowden, Callum Turner, and Josh O'Connor are the three actors vying for the role—and it's anyone's guess who will get their hands on the hallowed tux. Heck, if The Hollywood Reporter’s recent dispatch is to be believed, it might even be Robert Pattinson or Nicholas Hoult.

However! You don't have to be a super spy to see that there are some clues pointing to a specific candidate—namely, one Callum Turner. Below, we've broken down the evidence.

Though he's currently filming the remake of Possession alongside Margaret Qualley and in pre-production for The Comebacker, 2027 isn't looking especially busy for Turner, one of Britain’s most in-demand young actors. We can assume that Bond 26 will go into production next year, so a wide-open schedule would prepare Turner for the intense, high-commitment shooting schedule that Amazon will likely stick to.

Turner recently stepped down as a director of Twentytwo Films Ltd., the production company that he founded last year with his now-wife Dua Lipa. Given that the final screen tests also supposedly took place in the last few days, do we think this could be a result of some complicated contract negotiations? Or maybe he's just freeing himself up even more?

This isn’t hard evidence, exactly, but an argument in Turner's favor. One thing that people noted about Daniel Craig when he was first announced as Bond was that he wasn't exactly a blockbuster actor, and his lineup mostly consisted of strong, independent films, really only including one that signaled he'd make a brilliant Bond: Layer Cake.

Craig then, indisputably, went on to be one of the best-loved 007s to date, and we think that Turner's range over the past ten years makes him a pretty chameleonic, versatile candidate for the role. Across the likes of Green Room, Rose of Nevada, and Rosebush Pruning, we've really seen Turner take on all sorts of mystery, thriller, and action films. With Denis Villeneuve at the helm, there's no way that Bond 26 will be a simple spy story, and Turner's ability to take on all kinds of genre and tone makes him the perfect partner for whatever Villeneuve's vision will be.

Babies may be small, but their wish lists are anything but simple. They speed through milestones overnight and grow by the minute, making baby gifting particularly challenging.

When my daughter was born, we were showered with gifts—from onesies to chic outfits, rainbow colored toys, and an endless amount of books. #6920. SNL’s You Can See Everything Parody with Druski Resurrects a Classic Eddie Murphy Bit

Back in 1984, Eddie Murphy had fun with John Howard Griffin’s 1961 book Black Like Me with a Saturday Night Live sketch titled White Like Me. In it, Murphy caked on makeup to pass as a white man, flipping his world upside down. It’s still pretty funny to watch after all these years, even if putting on makeup to resemble another race for a joke is walking a tightrope. So it’s damn impressive that Saturday Night Live just got away with it again, with Druski.

On the most recent Saturday Night Live, guest star Druski was at the center of one of the night’s best sketches (and maybe the only one worth watching), which was also the inevitable parody of You Can See Everything, titled Dru Can See Everything.

The premise is simple: What if, before Nathan Fielder, Druski first filmed the final days of freedom for Elizabeth Holmes? Cast members Ben Marshall and an incredible Jane Wickline co-star and elevate the sketch from a mildly funny “haha” to “holy shit they’re actually committed here.” Clocking in just four minutes long, the sketch runs through the highlights of You Can See Everything’s first hour: Elizabeth (Wickline) and her partner Billy (Marshall) living at home, cooking eggs, showing off the controversial Theranos machine, and even Billy’s barenaked shower. But the one change is swapping out Fielder for Druski. And it makes a huge difference.

You can see everything (the sketch) below.

While Fielder’s social awkwardness and determination to ensnare a charlatan without falling victim himself is part of the movie’s drama, SNL knows that Druski would just call out Holmes and Evans’s bullshit straight up. No hesitation. “I just want people to see me as an ordinary person. Can you do that, Druski?” asks Wickline’s Elizabeth Holmes.

Says Druski, “Based on what I’ve seen so far, I’mma go ahead and say no. Y’all got some of the weirdest, freakiest vibe I’ve ever seen.” Grabbing the bowl of eggs, he then turns to the camera: “Whatever they said this bitch did, she done did that sh-t.” (Remember the eggs scene is like, ten minutes into the movie.)

Funny as Druski is, the real MVPs are Wickline and Marshall, even if all their jokes are about the size of Elizabeth and Billy’s saucer-pan eyes. The now spoofed-to-death “I’m always real” scene gets its SNL roast too, with Druski asking literally point-blank, “Are you trying to f-ck me?” Then, when Elizabeth shows off Theranos’s supposedly miraculous machine, Druski doesn’t hold back. “Listen white girl, I’mma say this with all love, right? This the most Temu ass piece of sh-t I done ever seen in my life.” Cut to “Billy” and his weird lizard.

(By the way, we know Saturday Night Live’s production design is top-tier, but it’s frankly amazing they were able to build a virtually precise replica of Holmes’s oceanfront rental and recreate the lighting conditions to make the shots look passably real. Remember that they shoot these things in under a week. I demand a behind-the-scenes video of how they pulled this one off.)

The sketch peaks at the end, when Druski emulates Fielder’s method for staging reenactments by putting on White Chicks-style makeup (to better understand Elizabeth, of course). There’s no getting around it: the man looks freaky. Uncanny. He’s like Fiona from Shrek in a J. Crew sweater. Which makes the joke’s reveal a funny jump scare. Post-midterms, it’s honestly awesome that this kind of woke comedy is “back” (if it actually ever left). I’m not smart enough to suss out if whiteface is actually acceptable. I just know Druski and the SNL team killed it this week.

From sharp knits to travel-ready extras, these are the pieces worth putting on your list.

#6917. Why I’m Still Turning to Oils for Thinning Hair

Much like the internal debate over whether to get curtain bangs or dramatically chop off all your hair into a bob, there comes a point in everyone’s life when they start considering using an oil for thinning hair. Personally, I started my own hair-oil journey after noticing sparse patches along my hairline. Many have praised these natural oils for their ability to not only treat breakage and damage but also to help hair grow back. So I figured, why not try these so-called hair miracle products?

While I personally noticed an improvement in my hair density, experts say to temper your expectations. “It is important to recognize that not all oils have been clinically proven to stimulate new hair growth,” says Marisa Garshick, MD, a board-certified dermatologist at MDCS Dermatology. “Rosemary oil and pumpkin seed oil have some preliminary research supporting their potential benefits, while others—such as castor, argan, and jojoba oils—are more beneficial for moisturizing the scalp, strengthening the hair shaft, and reducing breakage.”

Corinne Erickson, MD, a board-certified dermatologist and founder of Georgia Skin Specialists, considers them “adjuncts to medical management for hair loss, not stand-alone solutions. While topical minoxidil remains the gold standard for thinning hair, there are several oils with early data showing help for hair thinning that are generally safe when used as directed.”

Generally speaking, many hair oils are safe when used as directed, though some can cause irritation or allergic reactions. You may want to use them alongside other nourishing hair products for the best results. With all that in mind, these are the best oils to consider for thinning hair.

Of all the hair oils, rosemary is the most promising for thinning hair. Dr. Garshick explains that rosemary oil’s anti-inflammatory and antioxidant properties may support scalp health, and she cites studies suggesting it may help with pattern hair loss. Although evidence remains limited, she points to one small study in which rosemary oil produced similar improvements in hair count to 2% minoxidil after six months.

She and Dr. Erickson recommend Mielle’s Rosemary Mint Scalp & Hair Strengthening Oil, which mixes rosemary oil with castor oil, jojoba oil, and other botanical ingredients to treat breakage and nourish the scalp. “[It] is an easy, affordable option,” says Dr. Erickson. If you’re looking for something lighter, you can also try Dr. Groot’s Scalp Hair Thickening Serum, a serum made with rosemary, biotin, and caffeine designed to support scalp health and fuller-looking hair.

Dr. Garshick says that pumpkin seed oil contains phytosterols and fatty acids, which preliminary research suggests may inhibit DHT, a hormone implicated in pattern hair loss. “This may help explain [pumpkin seed oil’s] potential benefits for hair growth,” she says. However, much of the clinical evidence involves oral pumpkin seed oil supplements rather than topical applications, so it remains unclear whether applying the oil directly to the scalp provides the same benefits.

Dr. Erickson likes the Leven Rose Organic Pumpkin Seed Oil, which is pure and cold-pressed, with no fragrance or additives. “Promising, though not yet proven,” she says. You can also consider something like SVA Organic Pumpkin Seed Oil, another cold-pressed option you can easily massage into your hair as a pre-shampoo treatment.

Castor oil is another popular oil used in hair-growth treatments. Dr. Garshick says that although there isn’t strong scientific evidence that castor oil affects hair growth, it can help nourish your hair and scalp. She says that it is rich in a fatty acid called ricinoleic acid, which is known to add moisture and condition to the skin and your strands. “It may help reduce dryness and breakage, which can make the hair appear thicker and healthier over time,” she says.

Try something like the Fable & Mane HoliRoots Hair Oil, a pre-shampoo treatment that mixes castor oil with ashwagandha, an antioxidant-rich berry called amla, and a plant-root blend designed to strengthen strands and soothe the scalp. You can also try the Carol’s Daughter Goddess Strength 7 Oil Blend Hair & Scalp Oil, which blends castor oil with black cumin seed and six other nourishing oils to hydrate the scalp and treat breakage.

Avocado oil contains fatty acids and vitamin E, which can help moisturize and condition the hair. While there aren’t studies showing avocado oil directly stimulates hair growth, it may help create a healthy environment for hair to thrive. “By helping support a healthy scalp environment and strengthen the hair shaft, it may help hair appear fuller and healthier while minimizing breakage that can contribute to the appearance of thinning,” Dr. Garshick agrees.

She likes Kiehl’s Magic Elixir, a pre-shampoo treatment that combines avocado oil with rosemary leaf and safflower oil to moisturize the scalp and alleviate dryness. “It is especially useful for those looking to incorporate scalp care into their routine,” she adds, “while improving hair manageability, smoothness, and shine without adding another leave-in product.” You can also try the Grow Good Everything Serum, which has a nourishing mix of oils (avocado, coconut, sunflower seed, and more) to seal the cuticle and prevent split ends and breakage.

Argan oil is extremely nourishing for hair; it contains vitamin E, fatty acids, and antioxidants that protect hair from environmental aggressors that cause damage, keeping hair feeling smooth and hydrated. Dr. Garshick says that although it hasn’t been proven to stimulate hair growth, it can improve hair softness, reduce frizz, and minimize breakage, which helps hair retain length and appear fuller.

She recommends something like the Augustinus Bader The Hair Oil, which combines argan oil with vitamin E, baobab and pomegranate oils, and a special complex that smooths out frizz and treats breakage so hair looks—and feels—healthier and fuller. You can also try SheaMoisture’s 100% Argan Oil, which, as the name suggests, is made of pure argan oil to nourish and restore dry and damaged hair.

Are There Any Downsides to Using Oils for Thinning Hair?

Dr. Garshick says that while hair oils can help with dryness, breakage, or scalp issues, they might not be the best solution for hair thinning. She explains that some oils can cause irritation or an allergic reaction, while heavier oils can cause buildup or worsen scalp conditions.

“It is also important to remember that hair loss can have many causes,” she adds. Genetics, hormonal changes, nutritional deficiencies, and medical conditions could all be reasons your hair is thinning, so you may need a more effective treatment. For those noticing persistent shedding, a widening part, or thinning that is progressively getting worse, she recommends seeing a board-certified dermatologist to identify the cause and get the right treatment.

How Do You Use Hair Oils for Thinning Hair?

Dr. Erickson says you must always dilute essential oils in a carrier oil (like jojoba) to reduce the risk of scalp irritation. If you’re prone to flaking or scalp conditions like seborrheic dermatitis, she recommends skipping oils altogether.

You’ll generally just massage in your oil of choice to stimulate circulation on your scalp. Start slowly to see how your hair and scalp tolerate it, then increase frequency up to a few times a week. Dr. Garshick says dermarolling may help by creating micro-injuries that activate wound-healing pathways and growth factors, promoting healthy hair growth. But you’ll want to be careful, as there’s a risk of irritation, allergic reactions, and inflammation. So when in doubt, ask your doctor.

When we test and review a product, we take a holistic approach to deliver well-rounded product recommendations. First, we lean on Vogue’s vast network of experts—from board-certified dermatologists to celebrity estheticians—to gain professional insight into the industry’s standout products, ones these specialists would actually use on their clients. We pair their expertise with our editorial best practices to curate the thoughtful edits you read on our site.

For oils for thinning hair, we selected the best based on the following characteristics: oil type, featured ingredients, texture, ease of application, finish, and the hair-care concerns they address. #6913. The Best Statement Coats and Jackets for Winter 2026—And How to Style Them

It’s been a good year for bold trends. We’ve had color (red, purple, cobalt), we’ve toyed with print (I won’t prattle them off; it’s the entire animal kingdom), we’ve embraced embellishment. It was only a matter of time then—literally speaking—for novelty coats to take off. Outerwear is essential this time of year, and for the fashion-motivated, nothing anchors your outfit quite like a statement jacket.

A trend defined by its opulence—think sumptuous satin, lustrous velvet, floral brocade, and even feathers—these statement-making toppers are leading the outerwear narrative for the season. They’re cropped in some of fashion’s typically minimalist-leaning collections (The Row, Kallmeyer, and Róhe), and have been a signature of CFDA Emerging Designer of the Year winner Colleen Allen’s designs. An if-you-know-you-know fashion find, her velvet housecoat has earned the attention of Jennifer Lawrence, who stepped out in the brand’s chartreuse version earlier this fall.

Styling-wise, the novelty coat anchors dressy looks just as effortlessly as it does casual ones. Take Chan Luu’s floral-printed duster for example: It adds an undeniable pop to a basic T-shirt-and-jeans combination—especially when said staples come courtesy of Ossou and fellow CFDA nominee Emily Dawn Long. But with the holiday season fast approaching, these bold pieces also make for festive outfit complements, whether paired with an LBD (see Staud’s) or slinky satin trousers (see The Row’s). Our guide on the styles to buy—and how to wear them—below.

When Jennifer Lawrence stepped out in Colleen Allen’s now-sold-out chartreuse velvet housecoat, the trend officially made the leap from runway to street. Her understated styling is why it works: simple black trousers, velvet slippers, and oversized shield sunglasses. While it might not be Lawrence’s exact version, Reformation’s satin car coat in the same striking hue works just as well.

Give your next off-duty look a fashion-forward upgrade with Chan Luu’s floral-printed duster coat. Create contrast with staples that may appear basic but are anything but—think Ossou’s sleek blue jeans and Emily Dawn Long’s graphic tee. Finish with accessories that pack just as much punch: Staud’s crushed-velvet mules, Khaite’s zebra-print hobo bag, and Chan Luu’s smoky-quartz beaded necklace.

Everyone has a go-to black jacket in their rotation, but this winter, why not make it The Row’s flirty feathered find? It pairs beautifully with everything satin trousers and leather ankle boots. For a night out, qe Calvin Klein’s dainty silk bra underneath, sweep your hair back with Lié Studio’s sleek silver barrette, and end on a pop of color; we love Hunting Season’s navy satin clutch here.

A classic LBD is one of those wardrobe heroes that goes the distance, no matter the season. Cos’s square-neck mini has caught our eye as something to wear it to more than a few festive outings. Styling-wise, Staud’s floral-brocade jacket takes the look to new heights, especially when paired with Dôen’s strappy heels and Celine’s sleek cube handbag. Drape Maison Magdalena’s fringed silk scarf around your neck or shoulders for a delicate finish.

Of all the jewel tones to choose from this season—trust us, there are plenty—Posse’s gold-foil jacket truly dazzles. Show it off on your next night out with La Ligne’s black satin maxi skirt, The Row’s little white kitten-heel pumps, and Le Sundial’s tassel-adorned velvet pouch. As for accessories, turn to a pair of sculptural earrings, also from Le Sundial, and Nili Lotan’s floral brooch for one final dose of fashion flair.

#6911. The 12 Best Puffer Jackets You Can Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We love a barn jacket. We love a blazer. But when it’s below freezing and snowing sideways, neither is going to do you much good. That’s puffer jacket weather, and no number of layered sweaters under your favorite jacket is going to change the forecast.

Luckily, there’s no shortage of puffer jackets out there. The trick is finding one that’s warm enough, durable enough, and ready to stand up to whatever precipitation the winter sky throws its way—all while looking good enough that you don’t feel like the Michelin Tire Man every time you leave the house. The good news? There are plenty of puffer jackets that fit the bill, from luxury options from Canada Goose and Moncler to more affordable ones from REI and Carhartt.

There is a range in how exactly a puffer gets its shape, too. Most are down-filled, and the way the stitching is designed affects the puffiness. The fewer the stitches, the bigger the puffs. For something more packed down, there are ones with denser stitching that make for a slimmer-fitting jacket. Then you get into the nitty-gritty: Is the hood removable? Can you wash it at home? Is it really, truly, waterproof? Start your shopping with these puffer jackets you can buy right now and wear for many cold days to come.

One of the most iconic purveyors of the puffer jacket, TNF is a brand that deserves the hype it gets. This style, with its wide puffs, is a throwback look that’s oh so welcome in the 2020s. It's designed to tough out even the coldest winter days, with a water-repellent and windproof outer, goose down, and a three-piece hood that you can stow away into the collar.

To me, this is the perfect packable puffer: Slim enough to roll up and stash away and sturdy enough to brave wind, rain, and snow. REI designs this lightweight style for the man on the go, so for all your winter hikes and outdoor excursions, this is the perfect in-between option that won't weigh you down.

Canada Goose is a household name when it comes to winter gear, and the Lawrence Puffer is one of its best sellers for good reason. Our editors (along with actors like Daniel Craig and Ethan Hawke) love this lightweight, warm, cozy jacket, which was designed with the details in mind; it has a suede chin guard so the stand collar won’t bother you, backpack straps, reflective stripes, and even two different color options for the disc logo.

Eddie Bauer’s CirrusLite is a sleeper hit. It won’t win awards as the most wind-resistant or coolest looking, though I love the more earthy colorways. It layers well, keeps you warm, and is durable enough if you need to wear it on a legit mountain—all this while being super lightweight and easy to pack.

Whether you're commuting in a bustling city or packing for a cold-weather trip, this lightweight jacket is a winter staple. It's stylish but doesn't skimp on insulation for a chilly day. The best part, though: Unlike many other puffers, this one is about as slim fitting as you can get. Kiss all the extra bulk goodbye this winter. Or, if temperatures go below freezing, add a wool coat on top for extra coverage.

Not only is this jacket insulated, but the interior is lined with sherpa, too, making it a cold-weather superhero. The high collar and full-front zip make it virtually windproof, so whether you’re darting from building to building or staying outside for a while, you’re all set.

Don’t get me wrong, an Arc’teryx Thorium is a phenomenal jacket. I just think at that $450 price point, you’re often just as good going for a North Face Nuptse (if you want an outer layer) or an REI down jacket (for layering). I think Veilance, which is the higher-end and more fashionable Arc’teryx sub-label, is a more interesting proposition. You get a sleek city cut, a nicer matte ripstop shell, and insanely warm 1000-fill down.

Rocky Mountain makes one of the sickest jackets out there. Westernwear is on a bit of a downturn—well, it’s classic, but had its cultural peak two years ago—but you are seeing this style of working Western down jacket with leather yoke pop up more and more. Rocky Mountain Featherbed is the OG, starting in the 1960s as a real brand for cattlemen and their employees. Its current iteration is actually a Japanese-owned reproduction, which means it’s the real fucking deal. It looks cool, wears hard, and is probably warmer than anything on this list.

Lululemon is based in Canada, and maybe that explains why all of its outerwear is so good. We love its bulkier puffer jacket, but for a sleeker, winter athleisure feel, this minimally puffed jacket is great, too. It keeps the heat in and water out, and with a stretchy, down-filled construction, you’re sure to stay warm and cozy.

This puffer from Aritzia was made with the same attention to detail and tech that goes into high-performance sleeping bags. It has 700-fill down, but it’s sewn with big horizontal chambers like a sleeping bag. It’ll withstand temperatures as low as -22°F, so it’s the only coat you’ll need for surviving a serious cold snap. The fit is intentionally oversized—longer in the arms and broader at the shoulders—to accommodate bulky sweaters underneath. Plus, the Japanese fabric is both water-repellent and wind-resistant.

Is this an outdoorsy jacket? No. Is it outdoors capable? Yes, absolutely. Heavy down jackets—as real mountaineers use them—are made for base camp, when you’re not moving but need a lot of heat. This is perfect for that.

The issue with most puffer jackets is that they’re not made to be especially abrasion or weather resistant. These jackets were originally made for cold and dry days on mountaintops. But Filson takes a different approach.

The Down Cruiser shell is a classic heavy oil-finished Filson cotton. That makes this jacket more weather-resistant and abrasion-resistant than any other option on this list.

So, you want the best? For performance, you can buy something cheaper from an outdoors brand. For fashion, you can get something cool and garment-dyed from Stone Island. For a merging of those sensibilities, Moncler is the only brand—the OG that made jackets for Europe’s mountaineers—that you should be buying from.

The Grenoble name denotes Moncler’s high-end ski line. Go to a nice ski resort in the Alps, and you’ll see plenty of these. Nothing looks as good or insulates as well. And if you don’t ski but want something fashion-forward, I still prefer the Grenoble line. The abrasion-resistant fabrics on the jackets look a lot better than the high-shine nylon that Moncler and other brands like to do on puffers.

While finding the perfect puffer jacket isn’t rocket science, there are a few key features you should focus on to narrow your search down from the 20 options above.

Insulation: Some climates call for the most intense down fill power there is, and some simply don’t. You don’t want to overheat this winter, nor do you want to be too cold, so go for a puffer with fill or insulation that your climate calls for. Don’t make the mistake of buying an 850-fill-power puffer if you live in a city where the coldest it ever gets is, like, 59 degrees.

Features: Not all puffer jackets are made the same. Personally, I need a removable hood. Look for design features including adjustability at the hem and hood, windproof fabric, zippered or buttoned pockets, a mock neck, and more, depending on your preferences.

Puffiness: If you’re someone who travels a lot and needs to stash your puffer in a suitcase from time to time, going for a super-puffy option probably isn’t going to be your best bet. Instead, look for puffers with a little less, well, puff, which will make them much easier to stow and pack. Some on this list even come with their own stow bags.

Being based in New York City means we know what makes a good puffer jacket. The options we listed above bring the heat in terms of functionality and style, all season long. Over the years, our editors have tried hundreds of styles and brands. Narrowing it down to our 20 favorites was no small feat, but these are just the picks that we swear by and return to time and time again for our winter wear.

Fill power is the number that tells you the quality of down in your jacket. The number technically indicates how many cubic inches of loft a single ounce of down has, but for simplicity, just focus on whether the number is relatively high or low—the higher it is, the higher-quality the insulation is: 600 is good, 700 is great, and 800 is spectacular.

Over time, as down goes through washing machines, tough weather conditions, and daily life, it can lose its fluffiness and slightly deflate your puffer. It’ll still keep you warm and dry, sure, just not quite so puffy.

#6907. The 5 Best Aluminum Carry-ons You Can Buy

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Tumi and Rimowa lead the pack, but the competition is getting close.

If you’re serious about travel, an aluminum carry-on is worth the investment. A well-made one can outlast soft-sided bags made from nylon, polyester, or canvas, as well as hard-sided suitcases made from polycarbonate—just a high-strength plastic. And it can withstand pretty much any weather: While water can seep through a traditional zipper, most aluminum-frame suitcases use metal latches and a compressed gasket to help seal out the elements, so your belongings stay safe and sound.

A full aluminum-shell carry-on typically uses high-grade aluminum-magnesium alloy sheets, reinforced corners, and those mechanical latches that now usually have TSA locks on them. Your stuff has a bodyguard. The catch? That bodyguard is a touch heavier than its polycarbonate counterparts. Full-aluminum carry-ons can weigh ten pounds or more before you’ve packed a single pair of underwear, and their shells have little give. That can be an issue if you’re taking an out-of-the-way flight and end up somewhere with carry-on weight limits.

But what likely catches your attention when you see an aluminum suitcase in the airport is the look. It’s got the retro-futurist swagger of the golden age of aviation with a modern luxury twist (and, yes, a higher price tag). There’s a reason Rimowa’s shiny luggage has become somewhat of a status symbol. Yours will probably pick up some nicks and dings along the way, but think of them as proof that it’s been places—and that you have stories to tell from all your travels.

Ready to invest in an aluminum carry-on for your next adventure? Make it one of our favorites below.

Tumi luggage comes with a hefty price tag (we told you these would be expensive!) but it truly will last for a very long time. When it comes to material, it doesn’t get more durable than this aircraft-grade aluminum, from which the exterior of this suitcase is made. Even the three-stage telescoping handle is made of aluminum.

But what’s even better about this carry-on is that, if you do end up needing more room (and the hard outer shell won’t budge because it’s just that damn sturdy), you can expand it and add two more inches of depth—yes, that means you probably won’t be able to use it as a carry-on after expanding it, but it’s a good option for when you want a smaller checked bag or like having the ability to pack more on your way back.

We can’t talk about aluminum carry-ons without mentioning the most famous of them all: Rimowa. Yes, it’s expensive, and yes, it will probably get a few dents over time. But Rimowa’s aluminum luggage is the best of the best in terms of style, durability, and recognition. Take it from Esquire’s senior director of commerce and partnerships, Krista Jones:

“When it comes to investment pieces, it all boils down to one argument. Why buy the mid-range level when the top-level is better quality? If you want a suitcase that will last you a lifetime—and this will—why spend $500 once on something you have to replace in five years? You might as well spend [$1,550] on something you don’t have to replace again. Rimowa offers that with a lifetime guarantee and will repair your bag too.”

When it comes to style, Away takes the cake for me. I’ve always loved the simplicity of the brand’s signature look with clean lines and smooth, rounded corners, and that look translates beautifully onto the aluminum version of Away’s classic carry-on. The shell is made from premium anodized aluminum, and the handles have gorgeous leather detailing—and the included leather luggage tag is a nice touch. This carry-on is also proof that aluminum suitcases don’t always have to come in silver. Yes, the silver colorway is a classic, but if you want something different, I’m obsessed with this deep metallic blue.

Earlier this year, Carl Friedrik joined the conversation and launched an aluminum luggage line of its own. In keeping with the brand’s Scandinavian minimalist roots, the carry-on has a simple aluminum exterior with leather detailing—but inside, it has a lot more going on. The interior boasts a zipped garment compartment on one side and an open section with a compression pad and straps on the other, as well as two zippered pockets for all the small things you want to keep organized.

Want a true aluminum carry-on without the $1,000 price tag? Nobl makes one for under $400, if you can believe it. It doesn’t have the glitz and glam of the Rimowa, but it works just as well and genuinely holds up. Plus, it comes with a free Nobl Air, a trackable chip that goes in your carry-on so you know where it is at all times—just in case you have to gate check it at the last minute.

#6903. How 12 Famous Watches Got Their Nicknames

From the 'Steve McQueen' Rolex to the 'Holy Grail' Omega.

A few weeks ago, Casio unveiled a new range of fully analogue G-Shock watches, billed as its biggest launch in decades. But what immediately caught collectors' imaginations wasn't the technology inside the new 2500 Series. It was the shape of the cases, whose rounded rectangular outlines bear more than a passing resemblance to the Patek Philippe Nautilus.

There’s precedent here. When Casio’s eight-sided GA-2100 appeared in 2019, enthusiasts immediately clocked its likeness to Audemars Piguet's Royal Oak and christened it the 'CasiOak'. The nickname stuck and has since become shorthand for the entire family. (Perhaps this is what happens when you launch watches with names like GA-2100.)

So what to call the new Nautilus lookalike? Suggestions have included “Nauty-Less” and “CasiNau,” but “CasioNaut” seems to have won out.

Elsewhere in recent watch news, Seiko has been having fun with another nickname. Most watch fans will be familiar with the term “panda,” used to describe a chronograph with a white main dial and two black sub-dials, an arrangement said to resemble the face of the bamboo-munching bear. Seiko's new Power Design Project Panda takes the resemblance to its logical conclusion, with actual panda eyes, ears and, even a little wagging tail on the case.

And next week, Piaget will release a new version of its Andy Warhol watch, a name collectors had been using for decades before the brand finally made it official in 2024, following an agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation.

Clearly, the peculiar business of giving watches nicknames is alive and well. Which gives us an excuse, as if one were needed, to run through a dozen great watch nicknames and the stories behind them.

Some are named after astronauts, actors, and models. Others have more unusual stories, involving a collaboration that never happened, a celebrity who almost certainly never wore the watch named after him, and an American president who wasn't even allowed to accept his watch.

Piaget ref. 15102 and its modern descendants

In the 1970s, Piaget was making some of the most extravagant watches money could buy, with chunky gold cases, colorful stone dials, and an impressive list of celebrity customers. Among them was Andy Warhol, who owned at least seven Piagets, including a large, cushion-shaped gold model, reference 15102, which he bought in 1973. (Four of his watches are now in Piaget’s collection.)

Six years later, Warhol met Yves Piaget, the founder’s great-grandson, and the two became friends. Warhol joined the Piaget Society, the glamorous circle of artists, designers, and celebrities cultivated by the company.

Introduced in 1972, the 15102 was produced for just five years before disappearing from Piaget’s catalogue. The design returned in 2014 under the name Black Tie, although collectors had long since nicknamed the original the “Andy Warhol.” Ten years later, the company finally made the name official, signing a licensing agreement with the Andy Warhol Foundation for the Visual Arts in 2024.

Piaget has since developed a collection bearing the artist’s name, with designs that draw on his taste for unconventional colors and materials. Among the first was a Clou de Paris edition featuring a blue meteorite dial surrounded by rows of tiny gold pyramids.

Warhol’s association has done vintage prices no harm, either. In May this year, an example resembling his own sold at Sotheby’s in Geneva for CHF166,399 – about $200,000.

Omega Speedmaster ref. 311.30.42.30.01.004

The Omega Speedmaster “Tintin” takes its name from a collaboration with the Belgian cartoon character that never quite happened.

In 2013, Omega released a version of its Speedmaster Professional chronograph (officially called the Racing) with an unusual red-and-white checkered pattern around the edge of its black dial. It was inspired by the rocket in Hergé’s Tintin adventures Destination Moon and Explorers on the Moon.

The original plan was to produce the watch in partnership with the owners of the Tintin rights. Early prototypes even featured the rocket on the dial, with a special case back to match. But the deal fell through, and Omega went ahead without the Tintin branding, leaving a rather curious design detail whose inspiration the company couldn't openly celebrate.

The story came to light when Robert-Jan Broer, founder of the watch website Fratello, spoke to Omega’s head of product development. Once collectors understood the connection, the “Tintin” nickname stuck. Illustrations from the comics accompanying Fratello’s coverage were subsequently removed at the request of the rights holder.

That said, the owners weren't opposed to putting the character on a watch altogether. Omega’s Swatch Group stablemate Swatch had already produced two official Tintin models for the character’s 75th anniversary in 2004, including a Happy Birthday Tintin edition limited to 9,999 pieces. Omega simply never managed to get its own collaboration over the line.

Seiko 6139, particularly the yellow-dial 6139-6005

Omega may have the Moonwatch, but Seiko can lay claim to the first automatic chronograph worn in space. And unlike the Omegas issued by Nasa, the Seiko in question was bought by the astronaut himself.

In June 1972, US Air Force pilot William Pogue paid $71 for a yellow-dial Seiko 6139 chronograph. He was training for NASA’s Skylab program and needed a watch to time engine burns. Like other astronauts, Pogue was due to receive an Omega Speedmaster, the chronograph Nasa had qualified for spaceflight, but his hadn't yet been delivered.

By the time his Omega arrived, however, Pogue had become so attached to the Seiko that he decided to take it into space as well.

When Skylab 4 launched in November 1973, the Seiko travelled in a pocket of Pogue’s spacesuit. During the mission’s 84 days in orbit, he wore it on his left wrist, with the Omega on his right. (The Seiko remained set to US Central Time.)

For years, the Seiko’s part in space history went largely unknown. Then American watch collector David Bruno spotted a reference to it in a mission transcript and contacted Pogue to find out what he'd worn. In 2008, the astronaut consigned the watch through the Astronaut Scholarship Foundation. It sold at Heritage Auctions for a surprisingly modest $5,975, complete with its original receipt and Pogue’s handwritten account.

The original Seiko 6139 has long been discontinued, but yellow-dial examples are now widely known as “Pogues.” They're still relatively affordable, too. Seiko has since revisited the look with the solar-powered Prospex SSC947, but it's an interpretation rather than a direct reissue.

There is one problem with the Rolex Explorer II colloquially known as the “Steve McQueen.” Nobody has been able to establish that Steve McQueen ever wore one.

His Rolex of choice was a Submariner. Yet the Explorer II association has become so firmly established that even Sotheby’s used the nickname when offering an example in May 2022, while the description below acknowledged that no convincing evidence existed for the connection. (Who first decided to attach the actor’s name to the watch remains a mystery.)

In fact, the same model has another nickname with an easier-to-fathom explanation. Freccione is Italian for “big arrow,” referring to the whopping orange 24-hour hand that dominates its dial.

Introduced in 1971, the Explorer II was designed for cave explorers and others who might spend extended periods without natural daylight. Its extra hand completed one revolution every 24 hours, allowing the wearer to distinguish day from night.

With its fixed steel bezel, busy dial, and that unmistakable orange hand, the original Explorer II looks quite unlike most of Rolex’s better-known models. It has since become a firm favorite with collectors.

In 1987, Omega introduced a Speedmaster that put a new spin on the familiar Moonwatch. The ref. 376.0822 featured an automatic movement, a day and date display, a 24-hour indicator, and an additional hand to count elapsed minutes. It made for a busy, rather technical-looking watch, and one that wasn't destined for a long production run.

Its relative scarcity would eventually catch the attention of American collector and Omega expert Chuck Maddox.

In 1999, Maddox was offered a 376.0822 in Germany for around $600. At roughly the same time, another Speedmaster appeared for sale in Iowa at a similar price. Faced with a choice, he went for the Iowa watch, reasoning that dealing with a seller he could visit in person was less risky than buying from overseas.

Almost immediately, he regretted passing up the German example. Finding another proved far more difficult than he had expected. A watch appeared in Japan with a price tag of more than $10,000. Another opportunity slipped away at auction. Eventually, with help from a fellow enthusiast, he managed to track one down.

Maddox later wrote about his hunt on his website and in online watch forums, comparing his obsession with finding the elusive Speedmaster to King Arthur’s search for the Holy Grail. (Watch collectors have never knowingly undersold a story.) He began referring to the watch simply as “the Grail,” and the nickname caught on among fellow collectors.

It's a tale that will be painfully familiar to anyone who's ever collected watches: the one that got away, and the lengths you'll go to for a second chance to spend a load of money.

When quartz technology arrived in the late 1960s, its promise was greater accuracy and, eventually, affordable watches for almost everyone. Rolex, naturally, took a different path.

Its first quartz watch, launched in 1970, was available exclusively in gold. The white-gold version of the ref. 5100 cost 18,300 Swiss francs before tax. According to Rolex expert James Dowling, that was roughly the price of a Porsche 912 and two-and-a-half times what the company charged for a Day-Date on its President bracelet.

Inside was the Beta 21, an early Swiss quartz movement developed jointly by a consortium of manufacturers. The technology was remarkable enough, but the watch looked like no Rolex that had come before it. Its large, angular case, broad bezel and integrated gold bracelet were a world away from the familiar designs of the period.

Italian collectors took to calling it the “Texano,” or “Texan,” presumably in recognition of its considerable size and extravagance.

Only around 1,000 numbered examples are thought to have been made, mostly in yellow gold. More than half a century later, the “Texano” remains as rare and extravagant as ever.

Nothing to do with glamorous magazine models, as it happens. The nickname comes from a book published in 2009 by Zenith historian and collector Manfred Rössler, who chose a photograph of the El Primero A3818 for the cover of Zenith: Swiss Watch Manufacture Since 1865. His reason for picking that particular watch? He simply liked blue dials. Fair enough.

Introduced in 1971, the A3818 was a 37mm steel chronograph with an angular case housing Zenith's celebrated El Primero movement. Its vertically brushed blue dial featured contrasting sub-dials and distinctive shark's-tooth markings around the edge.

Only 1,000 examples were made, according to Zenith, and the model had long since disappeared from production when Rössler's book brought it back to collectors' attention. Zenith revived the design in 2020, with a lightweight titanium Airweight edition following in 2022. Both had “Cover Girl” as part of their official names.

It doesn't get much more ’60s than Finnish fashion model Nina Rindt, married to Austrian Formula One driver Jochen Rindt and a regular fixture in the paddock during the sport's glamorous heyday. Photographs from the late 1960s show her watching races from the pit lane, often wearing a white-dial Universal Genève Compax chronograph on a wide leather cuff. The combination of glamorous wearer, racing connection, and period photographs proved irresistible to watch collectors, who began calling the model the “Nina Rindt.” Its black-dial counterpart acquired the even better moniker “Evil Nina.”

The nicknames helped transform the watch's fortunes. In the early 2010s, as collectors became increasingly interested in vintage chronographs with famous associations, prices for the “Nina Rindt” rose dramatically. As Eric Wind, founder of Wind Vintage and a former Christie's watch specialist, recalled: “That watch went from $3,000 ordinarily and was worth $40,000 within a year.” One particularly fine example sold at Christie's in 2015 for $47,500.

When Universal Genève relaunched in April this year, the Compax was central to its comeback, with new chronographs inspired by both versions. The watches now have automatic movements but retain the contrasting sub-dials of the originals—and are even offered on the wide leather cuff that helped make Nina's watch so recognizable.

In the 1983 film Octopussy, Roger Moore's James Bond wore a Seiko G757 Sports 100 that had been given a special upgrade by Q Branch. Its small circular digital display functioned as a tracking screen, allowing Bond to follow a transmitter concealed inside a Fabergé egg being sought by the film's villain, Kamal Khan.

Almost 30 years later, in 2012, Casio introduced the AE-1200, an inexpensive digital world timer with a striking resemblance to Bond's gadget. It had a similarly angular case, multiple digital displays and, most importantly, a little circular clock that seemed to recall the tracking screen on Moore's Seiko.

Enthusiasts spotted the resemblance and came up with a pun on Casino Royale, the 2006 Bond film starring Daniel Craig. Thus the “Casio Royale” was born, a nickname involving a different watch, a different manufacturer, and an entirely different Bond film.

The AE-1200 remains in Casio's catalog today, costing $55 and looking much as it did in 2012. Its miniature world map indicates the selected time zone, while the little circular display shows the time. It also has five alarms, a stopwatch, and a countdown timer. The Fabergé egg tracker is not included.

By 1972, Bulova was advertising the water resistance of its Oceanographer dive watch with the words “666 FEET” printed prominently on the dial. That's approximately 203 metres, although quite why the company chose such an oddly specific figure rather than a round 200 meters isn't entirely clear.

Given the number's rather ominous biblical associations, it didn't take long for enthusiasts to christen the watch the alliteratively pleasing “Devil Diver.”

The Oceanographer had other things going for it, too. The original was a handsome example of early-1970s dive-watch design, with a substantial cushion-shaped case, a rotating bezel, and a dial with extravagantly bold markers intended to be easily read underwater.

In 2017, Bulova teamed up with New York vintage-watch dealer Analog/Shift to gauge interest in reviving one of its archive designs. The Oceanographer came out on top. A memorable nickname with a whiff of danger presumably didn't hurt.

The watch duly returned in 2018, including an orange-dial edition limited to, naturally, 666 pieces. Since then, Bulova has produced smaller versions and an Oceanographer GMT capable of tracking a second time zone.

More than 50 years later, Bulova still prints “666 FEET” on the dial, although its official specifications now give the water resistance as a rather neater 200 meters.

Audemars Piguet Royal Oak Offshore ref. 25721

“You have spoilt my watch, you're a murderer!”

According to designer Emmanuel Gueit, this was Gérald Genta's reaction when he first saw the Royal Oak Offshore, a considerably beefier version of the Royal Oak he had designed for Audemars Piguet in 1972.

AP had been hoping to recapture some of the impact of the original Royal Oak, which had caused a sensation with its unusually large steel case, integrated bracelet, and extravagant price. By the early 1990s, the company wanted something to appeal to a younger audience, and Gueit was given the job.

His answer, launched in 1993, was the Royal Oak Offshore. The original's 39mm case grew to 42mm, enormous by the standards of the day. It was thicker, too, with a chronograph movement, rubber-coated crown and pushers, and a conspicuous gasket visible beneath the bezel. The Offshore name came from powerboat racing, a suitably muscular association for such an imposing watch.

The industry quickly christened it “The Beast.” Genta himself reportedly compared it to a sea elephant, which, frankly, would have made an even better nickname.

Even within Audemars Piguet, enthusiasm was mixed. The company's archive records that some insiders hoped the project would fail, while the first hundred examples had only Royal Oak engraved on their case backs, without the Offshore name. Hardly a ringing vote of confidence.

Customers eventually came around, helped by celebrity associations, particularly with Arnold Schwarzenegger. The Offshore went on to help establish the fashion for oversized luxury watches, which must have had Genta sobbing even harder into his fondue.

Omega Speedmaster Apollo XI ref. BA145.022-69

When Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin landed on the moon in July 1969, Omega had good reason to celebrate. Its Speedmaster chronographs had accompanied NASA astronauts throughout the Apollo program, and the company decided to mark the achievement by making 1,014 special gold editions.

These were considerably more luxurious than the steel watches issued to the astronauts, with 18-carat yellow-gold cases, bracelets, and dials, plus burgundy bezels. The first two were intended for President Richard Nixon and Vice President Spiro Agnew, complete with individual engravings.

There was just one problem. American rules governing gifts to public officials meant neither man could accept an expensive gold watch from a Swiss manufacturer. Both were returned to Omega, where they remain in the company's museum in Switzerland today.

Other recipients were luckier. Astronauts received their watches at a gala dinner in Houston in November 1969, while most of the remaining production was offered for public sale. Collectors subsequently nicknamed the reference the “Nixon,” after the president who never got to wear his.

The watches have since become highly collectible, particularly those with a connection to the Apollo astronauts. An example sold at Bonhams in 2024 for HK$384,000 [just under $50K], while Neil Armstrong's own gold Speedmaster, number 17, fetched $2.125 million in April last year.

Of course, Omega couldn't resist reviving the design, producing another 1,014 examples in its Moonshine gold alloy in 2019. But the originals have an appeal that can never quite be replicated, combining as they do mid-century space-age glamour with a whiff of Nixonian roguishness.

#6902. This Puget Sound Home Kept All of Its Original Charm—While Hiding Every Modern Convenience

The design team faithfully restored the details that have the most heart.

When this Puget Sound home was built in 1908, its original owners gave it a name: Heart’s Delight. Years later, after the house was bought by another family, principal designer Lisa Staton, alongside her senior designer Lauren Galeotti, took on the task of restoring the space with that same sentiment in mind.

“My favorite thing about this house is all of the love it holds,” Galeotti says. The new owners became deeply attached to the space, and the neighbors had long adored the house, too. As work progressed, the contractors and tradespeople fell for it as well. “She really charmed us all, from start to finish,” Galeotti says of the home.

That affection guided a renovation rooted in respect for what was already there. “Our passion for heritage homes is fueled by the original craftsmanship, history, and charm the homes hold,” Staton explains.

Every piece of original millwork stayed, and any new elements added had to take their cues from the old—even if that meant undoing some of the design missteps of the past. For example, a custom, made-to-match fireplace now sits over the original brick-and-plaster hearth that had been painted white. Though it was a whole team effort to get right, it now flawlessly complements the living room’s woodwork.

The current homeowners are also avid entertainers, so the kitchen needed a total strategic (and stylistic) rework that wouldn’t expand beyond its footprint. Adding the backsplash tile, created with Dorset Delft artist Aviva Halter, and a textural floor tile were key to giving the room its inimitable character. Other splurges, like all-new lighting throughout and the powder room's striking mural wallpaper, were necessary investments in the home's future.

But the biggest one, Staton says, was restoring all original details that everyone had fallen in love with: "always worth the investment!"

FAST FACTSDesigner: Lisa Staton and Lauren Galeotti of Lisa Staton Interior DesignStylist: Cozbi Jean Location: Puget Sound, WashingtonThe Space: A four-bedroom, four-bathroom Craftsman-style home built in 1908

This living room already “glows with daylight” thanks to its original windows, and the millwork has charm and texture in spades, so the main task in updating this room was to create a cozy, after-hours vibe.

“We wanted to make it warm and inviting, and every surface and corner needed to be enveloping,” Staton says. She achieved this by mixing newly sourced finds with well-loved pieces from the family’s personal collection, and it all blends seamlessly.

After entering the home through the original French doors, guests are greeted by the great room. This entertaining-ready space is faithful to its original style with two very notable additions: the custom fireplace cladding and accordion panels directly above that open to reveal the TV. It’s a room that can easily turn a quick drop-by into an entire evening in. “A pair of matching sofas invite you to sit and chat for a while,” Staton adds.

Keeping the spirit of this kitchen intact while gut-renovating it was a challenge, but the trick was to hide away the modern conveniences wherever possible and draw the eye toward period-appropriate accents instead.

“In heritage homes, it is really helpful to bring in antique elements to the space to add warmth and character in imperfect items,” Galeotti says. In this case, the flush mount ceiling lights, stools, window treatments, and hutch bring in texture and patina that add to the room’s authenticity.

Combining the storage and function of a kitchen island with the rustic character of a cook’s table, the kitchen’s central surface feels more like a piece of furniture than an extension of the cabinetry. “Since we did not need to put any appliances in the island, that really became an opportunity to keep beauty at the forefront,” Galeotti explains.

One of the team’s favorite parts of the kitchen, though, lies in the smaller details: the handmade backsplash tiles, which bring a personal, playful touch. “The client chose the subject matter of each custom-painted tile, including meaningful flowers and even their pets!” Galeotti says. Their Dorset Delft style makes the stoneware feel like it has always belonged in the house.

Though space is certainly tight in this pantry, it’s a workhorse area in its own right. “We designed custom cabinetry to line the walls with plenty of storage, and were even able to squeeze in a wall oven, microwave, and warming drawer,” Galeotti notes. This discreet appliance placement also helps the main cook space retain its ageless atmosphere.

The homeowners asked for this newly added powder room to feel “whimsical,” and Staton’s team granted that wish, selecting a dusky Andrew Martin mural wallcovering that depicts a landscape with traditional folk art motifs. Its towering pear trees, mythical creatures, and rambling vales read like an epic fairy tale across the four walls. “It is a small room, but we wanted to make a big impact,” she says.

According to Galeotti, “the dining room lives in the same footprint and largely looks exactly like the day it was built!” The team restored the original built-in cabinet, whose diamond-patterned panes echo the room’s leaded-glass windows. This clean-line geometry contrasts beautifully with the curves, plumes, and ruffled silhouettes seen in the window treatments and Murano glass chandelier.

Updating the primary bedroom’s layout was another necessary correction to previous design choices—the team took down a wall and turned two rooms into one. “However, given how the windows are on the large wall where the rooms used to be divided, it may have originally been one space,” Galeotti adds. Now, there’s an unobstructed view out to the water and mountains just beyond the property. They also added a his-and-hers closet, new flooring, and a bed Galeotti describes as a “perfect cloud” from which to greet the surrounding scenery each day.

Previously, the primary bathroom was completely open to the bedroom, with carpeting and a step up to access it. “It was a bit of a strange situation,” Staton admits. A serious layout rework was in order so that the space is now separate, level with the bedroom, and equipped with a freestanding tub and beautifully tiled shower. “We also added a feature we do in most bathrooms: use vintage or antique mirrors as doors to create medicine cabinets,” she adds.

“The addition of this bedroom at the back of the house gives guests a really private retreat in a quiet corner,” Galeotti says. With its bobbin bed frame, antique furniture finds, and misty color palette, this sleep space provides a soft place to land.

“This is the kids’ bathroom, but we imagined a bathroom that felt beautiful for children, guests, and grown kids alike,” Galeotti notes. The space originally suffered from the same “watch the step” quirk that the main bathroom did, so Staton installed a similar solution here: She put the plumbing into the floor and incorporated all-new finishes and fixtures to add elegant polish that all ages can appreciate.

Lisa Staton Interior Design is a boutique firm established in 2004 with roots in the East Coast and Seattle, united by a shared love for enhancing older homes and creating new ones inspired by historic craftsmanship. The team brings a unique blend of both modern and traditional elements to a wide array of homes.
