#6900. Tom Hardy’s Hair Through The Years—and How to Get Every Cut

Three decades, one head, a lot of styles. We’ll help you nail every look with advice from the pros.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can track Tom Hardy’s life through his haircuts. The floppy, past-the-ears noughties look. The bald Bane era. His Peaky Blinders slick-back. Then there’s the fade he wore on The Revenant press tour and the short-back-and-sides he’s settled into today. He’s been on a proper hair journey over the past couple of decades and, somehow, he’s looked great on every stop along the way.

Really, he’s done exactly what every man should do when it comes to hair. You should never have the same haircut at 60 as when you’re 16, so changing it up is essential. Sure, some, for Hardy, were for his roles. He probably wouldn’t have gone for the full shaved look if he hadn’t been cast as a masked supervillain who breaks Batman’s back. But, even so, his go-to now is a long way from the boyband look he wore when he was younger.

So, we asked some of London’s best barbers how to get each cut, as well as who each one suits. If you’re anything like Hardy, you’ll suit all six.

The haircut: Think of this as the slick-back’s more laid-back cousin. “It's a classic textured scissor cut with a low, natural finish,” says Robyn Mulgrew, a senior barber at Ruffians. “It’s kept soft around the ears and nape, with the sides relatively full and enough length to sweep it back.” No harsh fades or sharp lines, just a cut that looks like it grew naturally—and it’s a versatile shape that works for everything from oval to square and round faces.

What to ask for: Mulgrew’s advice? Keep it simple: “Don’t overcomplicate it, or you’ll confuse each other,” she says. “Use these key words: textured scissor cut with a low natural taper, swept-back top, styled to give a soft, lived-in finish.” All you need to do now is read it out with the help of a Hardy reference photo—job done.

How to style it: The idea is for your hair to look naturally lifted rather than quiffed. “I'd prep with Ruffians Beach Salt Spray, then blow-dry it in, sweeping the hair back and up,” says Mulgrew. “Keep the sides flatter so it still looks smart, then finish with out Matt Clay or Beach Fix for some texture.” Think swept-back beach hair, just the commuter’s version.

The haircut: Short, sharp, and no messing about, the soft buzz is for the guys who are confident with clippers. “This is pretty close to a buzz cut, approximately a grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a very subtle taper around the edges,” says Maxwell Oakley, co-founder of luxury London barbershop Supply 91. Fair warning, though: there’s nowhere to hide. “It’s quite an exposing haircut, so it works particularly well on someone with a strong hairline,” he says. Square and oval faces with a strong jawline suit it best—but Oakley’s not one for hard rules: “If you wear something with enough confidence, you can pull anything off.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber to use clippers rather than scissors,” says Oakley. “A grade three on top and a grade two on the sides, with a subtle taper through the edges; you could also mention to lightly break into the fringe with the clippers to keep the front looking natural and textured.” Though it might seem simple, it’s so precise that you need someone who knows exactly what they’re doing—so always stick to a good barbershop.

How to style it: Less is more. “Avoid anything wet or greasy, as that can make the hair look sparse,” says Oakley. “Keep things matte to bring out the natural texture, with just a little bit of hold; work a small amount of product through for some definition without weighing it down.” Basically, a tiny bit of paste rubbed between your palms and through your hair, and you’re out the door before you can say “buzz cut.”

The haircut: Brent Pankhurst has been cutting hair for more than 30 years, from training under Vidal Sassoon to his shop in the Thom Sweeney townhouse in Mayfair, so he knows a good crop when he sees one. "It’s disheveled elegance,” he says. “It has a relaxed, slightly rugged feel, but the foundation is a strong, carefully considered shape.” It suits most faces, too, as long as the proportions are tailored to you. “For gentlemen approaching their forties and beyond, I often prefer a little more length through the sides for a softer, less harsh finish,” Pankhurst adds.

What to ask for: It all starts with the temples: “The most important thing with this haircut—and, for me, every man’s haircut—is to cut a square shape through the temple area,” says Pankhurst. Ask for scissors throughout, with scissor-over-comb on the back and sides, which “gives the haircut better structure and helps it hold its shape as it grows.” You don’t want to be going to the barbers every couple of weeks, after all. The top should be cut with texture for movement and separation; if your hair is thick, ask for more texturing to take out some bulk.

How to style it: Work some styling conditioner through damp hair, then give it a quick blow-dry using your fingers. Finish with a nickel-sized amount of molding cream. “Spread it evenly between your palms, then work it vigorously through the hair for that rugged, disheveled finish,” says Pankhurst.

The haircut: The slick-back, minus the Wall Street energy. “This is a textured slick-back or flow cut,” says Chris Merrick, head barber at Murdock Soho. “Keeping it relaxed rather than tightly slicked allows the natural movement and texture to come through.” Basically, it should look like you’ve just run your hands through it rather than set it hours earlier. It suits most faces, too. “It works particularly well on oval, square and rounder face shapes; styling the hair back opens up the face and highlights the cheekbones, while some height on top can help elongate a rounder face.”

What to ask for: “Ask your barber for a textured slick-back or flow cut, with the sides slightly shorter than the top but enough length throughout to push the hair back,” says Merrick. “You want to retain movement and avoid a traditional short back and sides.”

How to style it: Towel dry your hair after showering, leaving it slightly damp, then work in a sea salt spray. “Blow-dry while using your fingers to guide the hair backwards and encourage natural movement,” says Merrick. Finish with a small amount of Murdock Matt Mud, worked between your hands and through the hair for a strong hold with a matte finish.

The haircut: Think of this as the haircut equivalent of rolling out of bed and looking like you meant to. “This is a classic textured cut with a messy, tousled finish,” says Guray Kesman, founder of luxury barbershop Kesman Grooming, “and it’s got that effortlessly undone look that Tom Hardy wears so well.” It’s also one of the most versatile cuts going: the fringe softens the forehead, so it suits oval, square, and longer faces, and it works best on straight to slightly wavy hair with some thickness.

What to ask for: “Ask for a scissor-cut textured crop, keeping the sides and back shorter but avoiding a harsh skin fade,” says Kesman. “You’ll want a good amount of length and movement through the top, with the fringe left slightly longer. The key is asking your barber to add texture rather than cutting everything too uniformly, so the hair has that relaxed, lived-in finish.”

How to style it: Work a small amount of lightweight styling cream or matte paste through slightly damp hair, then rough-dry it, directing the fringe forwards and slightly to one side. “Use your fingers to separate a few pieces,” says Kesman. “You don’t want it looking too polished or overly styled; a little movement is what makes this cut work.” When it looks like you’ve spent as little time as possible trying to perfect it, you’ve nailed it.

The haircut: If you were around in the ’90s, chances are you’re familiar with curtains—and you probably have the school photos to prove it. “To me, Tom Hardy’s curtains were something of an homage to Jonny Depp,” says Jay Hecker, master barber at Truefitt & Hill, the world’s oldest barbershop based in St. James’s, London. “We've seen this look before—it was everywhere when Brad Pitt broke though with Thelma & Louise and when David Beckham wore it in the ’90s—and you can trace it right back to old Hollywood’s Robert Mitchum.” Now, it’s making a comeback—and it suits most face shapes. “Because the hair is tucked behind the ears, face shape isn’t too much of a factor,” continues Hecker. “And if you want to accentuate your cheekbones, it’s definitely one to consider.”

What to ask for: “Ask for layers at the back to take out any weight, and keep most of the length on top so you can tuck it behind your ears,” says Hecker. A center part takes care of the rest. If you’re growing it out from a shorter cut, tell your barber so they can shape it in stages—just make sure you take a visual reference so you’re both on the same page.

How to style it: “Apply a cream like our C.A.R. cream or Circassian Cream to towel-dried hair,” says Hecker. “Then use a diffuser on your hairdryer on medium to low speed and heat for that textured finish.” The C.A.R. Cream has some history, too: Truefitt & Hill made it in the early 1900s for members of the Royal Automobile Club to keep their hair in check while driving open-top cars. Your day at the office, then, will probably be safe.

#6897. 6 Pieces of Wearable Tech That Solve Real-World Problems

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Health tracking, AI-powered notes, and more—behold, the most useful accessories on the market.

At this point, it’s normal to have a wearable, something like an Apple Watch or Fitbit, or an Oura ring to track your sleep and activity levels. You’ve maybe also heard of Whoop, pioneer of the screenless tracker. At the end of the day, all these devices do the same thing to varying degrees of success. If your focus is on training then the Whoop band is made for you, if you’re outdoorsy you have Garmin, and if you want to get better sleep maybe try Oura. But if you have other, more specific goals, it might seem like there’s not a lot out there for you. But worry not, all that is changing, and it’s changing fast.

These devices represent the latest breakthroughs in wearable technology, going beyond the basics and swinging for the fences. From rings and bands dedicated to blood pressure monitoring to an AI note-taker that you clip on your lapel, these aren’t trying to become your new all-in-one wearables. Instead, they are each trying to be a tool for those who need it most. With an eye toward mindfulness and focus, instead of overwhelming with data, these unique wearables go beyond just tracking sleep and activity level. These devices will make you think maybe the future really is here, and not so bad after all.

If you have one of many medical conditions (I genuinely do) that requires you to monitor your blood pressure you know how annoying those cuffs can get. Hilo suggests a better solution; Hilo Core is dedicated to providing users with insights into their blood pressure over time. As a result, the system is a bit more complicated than just a single wearable with an app. That’s the main part, but the FDA-cleared blood pressure cuff is the third part of the equation. The three aspects of the Hilo work in tandem to give accurate, consistent blood pressure readings with as little invasiveness as possible.

To start, you need to use the cuff to take baseline readings for a few days but after that you should only have to use the cuff once per month to calibrate your readings. After that, you can rely on the Hilo Band itself for accurate and consistent readings thanks to its advanced optical sensors. All of that is viewable in graphs and charts that put your hourly, daily, and weekly readings into a greater context.

Hilo isn’t the only company making wearables that are trying to replace chunky old-fashioned blood pressure cuffs. And unlike Hilo, Signal even does it without needing any kind of cuff at any point. All you need is the single Signal ring, and no, there’s no subscription to pay.

Compared to an Oura ring and other similar watches and bands, the Signal Ring uses more advanced sensors to capture your systolic and diastolic readings to display your blood pressure in real time. It’s not measuring general cardiovascular strain or anything vague like that, it’s actually measuring blood pressure. And like with the Hilo, same situation; you can view everything on the app.

It’s worth noting the device itself is not yet FDA cleared (and neither is the Hilo Band to be clear). A 451-person study the company commissioned did meet the requirements of ISO 81060-2, the international accuracy standard for non-invasive blood pressure devices.

This isn’t a watch or ring, but it definitely works best as a wearable. Plaud makes AI powered audio recorders that can also automatically transcribe everything for you. As a writer and editor, this is somewhere AI could have saved me time in my intern days.

The NotePin S is the size of a lapel microphone and clips right on to your shirt just like one too. At least that’s my favorite way to wear it—feels professional. You can wear it like a necklace or in a wrist band too, all options that are possible with attachments that come in the box.

I’ve written about vagus nerve stimulators before. It’s an underwritten trend, and the mindfulness tech that’s been coming out over the past year or two has been embracing it. Essentially, the vagus nerve runs from the brainstem all the way down to, really, every other major organ in your body. vagus nerve stimulation devices send electrical signals that can supposedly fix many ailments. In my experience, they are great for relaxation and can help with anxiety and stress relief. But really, it’s meant to aid your parasympathetic nervous system. This impacts your digestion, and is also the source of your “fight or flight” response.

Like other handheld devices I’ve used, you have to apply a layer of electricity conducting gel before a session with the Pulsetto. After that, put it on (it’s adjustable to any neck size) and let the electrodes work their magic. It’s certainly a well-built, futuristic feeling piece of tech, but as with any of these actual results may vary.

Pulse takes a different approach to smart rings. It’s not about becoming the best version of yourself by optimizing your sleep, fitness, and recovery every second of the day. Instead, this is a ring that senses your stress levels throughout the day and gives you vibrations (pulses) when you might, based on your vitals, need a breather. Be it meditation or just a minute away to get your composure, sometimes taking this time can be the difference between turning a bad day around and having it go worse.

Like the Pulsetto device, Sona is making a vagus nerve stimulator. This one goes on your ear like a single earbud. From there it sends stimulating signals to your vagus nerve, no goop required. Once you’ve onboarded the device and set up your protocol, the promise is that the AI is always adapting to you and your body’s rhythms.

Sona’s focuses are stress relief and better sleep. Yes, you are supposed to sleep with it on. Full disclosure, I can’t say I’ve tried this one and for nearly $1,000 I don’t want to recommend it wholeheartedly without hands-on time. But the promise is... well promising, compared to other similar devices out there, even if the price makes it currently unattainable for most.

This is far from all that’s out there. I’ve seen smart rings like Kiwear that control your phone with a flick of your finger. Or similar rings that let you control the display on your smart glasses. I’ve tried exoskeletons, like the upcoming Hypershell Halo, that genuinely feel like a robot is moving your body for you. And that’s just scratching the surface—we have a long way to go, and plenty of concerns to address along the way, but wearable tech isn’t going anywhere.

#6896. Stop Hunting for Pricey Antiques—Designers Say These 9 Humble Finds Give a Home So Much Character

They’re affordable, widely available, and stylish.

It’s any avid thrifter’s dream to stumble upon an extremely valuable piece of decor while browsing the shelves of their favorite antique store. But while you’re hunting for rare treasures, don’t discount the less-expensive finds—they can be the secret to a home that looks instantly filled with character.

“To me, a collected home is much more interesting when everything isn’t perfect or chosen for its monetary value,” designer Lauren Sullivan of Well by Design tells House Beautiful. “Often, it’s the less precious pieces that give a room its personality.”

Even as older generations are continuing to downsize and offload unwanted things to local thrift stores, it still might be hard to find the diamonds in the rough. Keep reading to find out what Sullivan, and a handful of other designers, say you should be on the lookout for on your next thrifting visit.

The most valuable antiques are the ones that have been passed down through generations. In Sullivan’s own bathroom, the designer keeps her great-grandmother’s vanity set on the countertop, its aged finish providing beautiful contrast with the more contemporary marble and finishes. In her bedroom, the same great-grandmother’s small bedside clock found a place on Sullivan’s nightstand.

“Neither is particularly valuable monetarily, but I love incorporating pieces that have been passed down through generations into a home filled with both old and new,” Sullivan says.

Designers love incorporating artwork into homes to add personality and dimension, but Sullivan, as well as designer Becca Meyer of BB Meyer Design, believe that antique shops are where you can really find some hidden gems.

“Some of my favorite pieces of art have come from flea markets or antique shops for very little money,” Meyer says. “I much prefer these to mass produced prints.” You’re more likely to find pieces with aged textures at these types of shops, and that patinaed quality creates an entirely unique look.

Sullivan explains that she’s actually particularly drawn to prints, as well as smaller paintings and silhouettes. She even has an inexpensive print of The Skating Minister in a vintage frame in one of her guest rooms, pictured above. “These smaller, or sometimes quirky, pieces can bring so much personality to a space without requiring a significant investment,” she adds. They may not be originals, but they’re definitely unique.

Old pottery and ceramics are widely available, and they are usually affordable as well. Designer Brooke Snyder of Design Hutch loves how they make any place feel more curated.

The imperfections these handmade pieces carry, from uneven shapes, variations in glaze, or evidence of years of use, are what help these items bring a warmth to a room, particularly when placed near more refined materials, according to Sullivan. They feel humble, maybe even utilitarian, especially when displayed in a kitchen. Or, better yet, used in a kitchen (as long as they’re safe).

“They're beautiful on their own but also so functional for holding utensils or branches or flowers,” Meyer adds. “They make any space feel a little more lived-in and loved and help tell a story—and they don't break the bank!”

“I love incorporating vintage plates, old copper pots, and woven baskets into a home,” explains designer Joe Waroquier. Though these are run-of-the-mill, everyday objects, they can take on and provide an entirely different life when displayed in the home as decor. Mismatched plates on a wall, a collection of copper pots hanging on a rod or resting on shelves in the kitchen, an old crock filled with branches—these items invite in warmth, texture, and more personality.

“I’m particularly drawn to pieces that show their age, whether it’s a little patina, a worn finish, or an imperfect glaze,” he adds. “I love mixing these finds with more contemporary furnishings because that contrast makes a room feel collected and personal rather than overly designed.”

Mirrors can be intriguing focal points of a room, or they can add dimension to a gallery wall. However you want to style them, Snyder loves finding these reflective pieces at antique malls and vintage shops.

“Purchasing something that you don’t see every day from a retailer allows this unique piece to stand out on its own, without feeling like something you see everywhere or feels too simple,” she explains.

As Snyder puts it, antique table lamps provide high visual impact with low investment. Adding one to your accent table or on a bookshelf is an easy way to make your space feel more curated while inviting in another level of ambience with the lighting.

These vintage lamps in the above space, for instance, were bought from a collector and make the primary bedroom feel much more charming and curated. And unlike antique sconces, there’s no need to hardwire these into the wall.

Besides pottery and lamps, designer Ryann Swan Hackett of Ryann Swan Design loves to find and collect other small tabletop decor for her projects. Think candleholders, vintage coffee table books, and trays—things that can layer a little history and individuality into a room or simple vignette.

“If you’re lucky,” Swan Hackett says of the vintage coffee table books, “you’ll find an old inscription inside, which gives the book a story of its own.”

In terms of midsize accent pieces, Meyer says that she has a soft spot for finding antique stools “because you can pop them into any space to add depth and dimension.” Tuck them beside an armchair in the living room, add them to an empty kitchen corner and place a plant on top, or stick one in a bathroom by the tub for somewhere to keep a drink while relaxing. And if you find an upholstered one that could use new fabric, switching it out for a pattern you love is probably worth it.

“These bring a natural warmth that can be difficult to replicate with something new, and their small size makes them fairly reasonable in price for a vintage furnishing piece,” Meyer says.

A larger piece of furniture is likely always going to be more expensive than a smaller one, but designer Yena Jung of By Yena Designs thinks vintage hutches are the versatile antiques people should pay more attention to. They come in many different shapes, styles, and sizes, and can easily make a home in any room of the house.

“A beautiful vintage hutch can add warmth, charm, and a sense of history to a space,” Jung says. “I especially love how their imperfections and natural patina make them feel collected over time.”

Follow House Beautiful on Instagram and TikTok.

#6895. The 25 Sexiest Movies on Prime Video Right Now

Including the best erotic thrillers, raunchy comedies, and all the mess in between.

Even in just the past decade, sexy cinema has come a long way from Fifty Shades of Grey. No judgement here—the entire trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video if you haven’t seen the Dakota Johnson and Jamie Dornan-starring franchise. But if you’re looking to expand your range a bit, we have a lot to recommend.

Sexy movies are having a comeback in 2026, thanks to Wuthering Heights, I Want Your Sex, and—another Olivia Wilde film—The Invite. While Emerald Fennell adapted Emily Bronte’s novel into a tortured and depraved sexual experience, Wilde’s I Want Your Sex and The Invite used sex more as a vehicle for dark comedy. But it’s not just new films on this list. We can also point you to Weekend, Ghost, Before Sunrise, and just any kind of sexy movie you’re looking for on screen.

Below, you’ll find our pick of the 25 sexiest movies you can stream on Prime Video right now.

Olivia Wilde and Cooper Hoffman star in Gregg Araki’s raunchy workplace romantic comedy about a high profile artist (Wilde) who ropes one of her new assistants (Hoffman) into a dominant/submissive relationship. At first, he enjoys the attention, but the rollercoaster of their sexual power dynamic eventually drives them both insane.

Sydney Sweeney does Rear Window meets Peeping Tom in this sexy crime drama about a couple who becomes obsessed with the sex lives of the people they can see from their apartment window across the street. The film also stars Justice Smith and Ahsoka’s Natasha Liu Bordizzo.

No Hard Feelings follows a down-on-her-luck Uber driver (Jennifer Lawrence) who is hired by a very wealthy couple to take their inexperienced nineteen-year-old son (Andrew Barth Feldman) on a date to boost his confidence before he leaves for college. The film treads dangerous waters, but Lawrence won over the audience and earned herself a Golden Globe nomination for the sexy comedy.

Love her or hate her, Emerald Fennell can make a steamy movie. Wuthering Heights is a little more of a reimagining than an adaptation, taking quite a few liberties with the text, but it puts the romantic and sexual dynamics right at the forefront of the story. And there’s no beating Margot Robbie and Jacob Elordi as the leads.

The Fault in Our Stars director Josh Boone teams up with It Ends With Us novelist Colleen Hoover for a romance drama about a teen pregnancy that alters the course of two lovers’ lives. That is, until later in life, when the couple (Allison Wiliams and Dave Franco) reunite to explore what could have been.

Emerald Fennell’s steamy The Talented Mr. Ripley–esque thriller, Saltburn, follows a young student at Oxford University who becomes infatuated (read: dangerously obsessed) with one of his aristocratic classmates, who invites him to his family’s grand estate. It’s a darkly funny and wildly intense ride.

The phenom that saved dark romance novels: Fifty Shades of Grey and the entire Fifty Shades trilogy is available to stream on Prime Video. The story, for those who still don’t know, follows a recent college graduate (Dakota Johnson) who signs a contract to take part in a sexual bondage relationship with billionaire Christian Gray (Jamie Dornan).

L’inconnu du lac (known as Stranger by the Lake) is one of the most acclaimed erotic thrillers of the 21st century, picking up Best Director and the Queer Palm awards in the year it premiered at the Cannes Film Festival. In Stranger by the Lake, Franck, a man in his 30s, meets Michel by a lake where gay men often bathe and immediately falls for him. What he doesn’t know, however, is that Michel is a psychopathic murderer. Stranger by the Lake is as smart as it is sexy, and it will truly keep you on your toes.

Who said rom-coms can’t be sexy too? Secretary charts the dominant-submissive relationship that blossoms between a lawyer and his secretary. It’s certainly on the crazier side and may not be for everyone, but Secretary is a brilliantly acted film that feels quite far ahead of its time for such a mainstream movie in its original take on a romantic BDSM relationship.

Carol does something a little different: It’s a slow-burning romance with sensuality bubbling beneath the surface. It follows a department-store worker and aspiring photographer whose life takes an unexpected turn when she falls in love with a mysterious, beautiful older woman going through a difficult divorce. Todd Haynes’s Oscar-nominated period romance is a masterpiece of modern queer cinema, with career-defining performances from Rooney Mara and Cate Blanchett. Hugely romantic and remarkably executed, Carol is a special film that’ll certainly stick with you.

Alongside Four Weddings and a Funeral, Sirens was one of the films that brought generational British heartthrob Hugh Grant to American audiences. Grant plays a progressive reverend who, with his wife, pays a visit to an eccentric artist whose work is viewed as blasphemous. His wife soon finds herself drawn to the three models working with the painter, and what ensues is a clever breakdown of 1930s sexual repression.

Everyone loves a meet-cute, and Weekend’s is as brilliant as they come. Russell thinks he’s signed up for a one-night stand with Glen, a man he meets in a club, but over the course of a single weekend, a deeper relationship quickly grows between the two. Touching, oddly realistic, and distinctly British in its sense of humor, Weekend is sexy, funny, and grounded in a way that few romantic comedy-dramas manage.

In Eternity, Elizabeth Olsen plays a woman who, upon reaching the afterlife, is forced to choose between two people to spend eternity with: her first husband, who died young, and her second husband of 65 years. Eternity is infectiously sweet, a little sexy, and an essential modern comedy.

It might be a stretch to call this one “sexy,” per se, but you won’t find a romantic drama quite like this one—and you definitely won’t find one that’s more intense. Luca Guadagnino’s Bones and All is a road-trip drama that follows a young woman who develops feelings for a disillusioned young drifter on a 3,000-mile journey across America. Oh, and they’re both cannibals.

Wong Kar-wai’s romantic masterpiece proves that a film can be truly romantic, sensual, and intense while still holding solidly on to a PG rating. In the Mood for Love follows a man and a woman who discover that their spouses are having an affair with one another, but as they bond, they begin to develop their own connection. This is one for the yearners.

Sexy films are by no means a new phenomenon, and Caligula is one of the originals. Though the original was pretty poorly received, the film recently received a restoration and rerelease that reconstructed the film entirely from footage that didn’t make the final cut.

Love triangles don’t get much more complicated than this, and sports dramas don’t get much sexier. Challengers follows two friends who fall in love with the same young tennis prodigy and the complicated chaos that ensues when their relationships get messy. Tennis has never looked so thrilling

Set in the dreamy Italian countryside, Call Me by Your Name remains the film that introduced Timothée Chalamet to the masses as a future leading man (and got him his first Oscar nomination). Here he plays a teenager who falls in love with his father’s assistant and comes to terms with his sexuality. The film is sad, symbolic, and deeply romantic.

The legendary leading duo of Patrick Swayze and Demi Moore speaks for itself. Ghost is maybe a little more on the sentimental side than some of the other “sexy films” in Prime Video’s lineup, but it’s a deeply moving one worth your time.

Portrait of a Lady on Fire was an immediate hit with critics and audiences when it was released in 2019. The film follows an artist commissioned to paint an aristocratic heiress, but things become more complicated when the two fall in love. Romantic films don’t get more French than this one, which is also loosely based on the timeless tale of Orpheus and Eurydice.

Nicole Kidman and Harris Dickinson are an absolutely electrifying onscreen pair in this erotic thriller that sees a CEO begin an affair with her power-hungry intern, putting the stability of her family life and career on the line. Kidman picked up the Best Actress award at the Venice Film Festival for her role, and it’s been called one of her most ambitious performances to date.

Can anything capture your heart like a meet-cute romance? American traveler Jesse and French student Céline unceremoniously meet on a train from Budapest to Vienna, deciding on a whim to spend his last hours on the continent together. It’s the first in Richard Linklater’s acclaimed Before trilogy, and it sets you up for a brilliant slow burn.

If a Skarsgård signed up for it, I’m interested. He’s about to play a love interest made out of wicker, but last year Alexander Skarsgård played the mysteriously sexy biker-gang leader Ray in Pillion. The romantic black comedy tells the story of a shy man who begins a dominant-submissive relationship with the aforementioned biker, and it traces his personal growth as he navigates their dynamic. Pillion is sexy, crazy, and downright hilarious. It’s British black comedy at its finest.

Starring the king of crazy, surprisingly sexy indie movies, James Spader himself, Sex, Lies and Videotape is a true cult classic. The film revolves around a married couple with intimacy problems whose lives are changed when a mysterious drifter with a peculiar interest comes to stay with them.

David Cronenberg’s notorious erotic thriller is perhaps the most transgressive entry on this list, following a group of people who become sexually fixated on car crashes. Adapted from J.G. Ballard’s novel and starring James Spader, Holly Hunter, and Deborah Kara Unger, Crash is disturbing, provocative, and about as daring as erotic cinema gets.

#6894. Duchess Meghan Looks Somber in Monochrome Black at the Launch of the Lost Screen Memorial in Brussels

She and Prince Harry held hands as they arrived at the installation, which is part of their ongoing campaign against online bullying

Prince Harry and Duchess Meghan presented a united front as they arrived, hand-in-hand, in Brussels, Belgium, today, to mark the opening of one of their Archewell Foundation Parents’ Network projects.

For her first public appearance since the unfortunate news regarding her father, Thomas Markle, Meghan looked sharp and polished. The duchess wrapped herself up in a luxurious black double-breasted coat that she belted tightly at the waist, pairing it with tailored trousers and pointy patent-leather stilettos.

Meghan’s vintage Dior crystal-and-black enamel door-knocker earrings (which she’s worn out on special occasions before) stood out thanks to her low bun, while her beloved Cartier Love bracelet decorated her wrist. For another pop of light, Meghan pinned a red poppy accent to her coat in honor of Remembrance Day, matching her husband.

Harry, in a navy suit, white shirt, and patterned black tie, never left her side.

The evening saw the opening of the Lost Screen Memorial, an art installation and global advocacy campaign that serves as a moving tribute to children and teenagers who lost their lives due to online harms.

At the event, held at the European Parliament, Harry gave a moving speech criticizing tech platforms’ addictive designs and highlighting the importance of protecting children’s youths. Children “deserve to feel safe wherever they are—in a classroom, on a playground—at any time, no matter where they are,” the prince said.

His comment came days after Meghan’s estranged father was arrested by the FBI and charged with one count of possession of child pornography.

Meghan released a statement regarding the ongoing child porn investigation involving Thomas some weeks ago, but has since stepped back from public engagements. She even reportedly opted out of making a speech at today’s event as she focuses on her wellbeing. “She’s just not strong enough, emotionally, to be doing any public speaking right now,” an insider told the Telegraph.

#6889. The Unexpected Benefits of Actually Calling Someone

Thirty minutes on the old blower is better than therapy.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The options for communication are endless. Getting in touch with someone has never been easier, which also means we are subject to frequent digital conversations we’d rather not have. You can thank our overlords in Big Tech for that. Friends, family, enemies, and strangers alike can pepper open in-boxes with questions and concerns ranging from the unnecessary to full-scale attacks on your character, tastes, and loosely held opinions. Nearly 90 percent of Americans use text messaging or messaging apps daily, with 29 percent saying they’re texting more than they were one year ago, according to a 2026 YouGov study. Even with notifications turned off, they lurk in the background, and the social pressure to respond is hard for almost anyone to ignore.

But there is an anecdote to this scourge of modern life.

As an older millennial, I am part of the generation that remembers when the Internet changed our lives, but I also experienced defining moments using a simple telephone. Even in the infant stages of the Internet, the humble telephone, attached via cord that plugged into a wall, was the conduit for good and bad news; it was how we made plans and how relationships grew and solidified. I spent hours with the handset cradled between my teenage double chin and shoulder, plotting to see bands play, staying up too late talking to girls, and calling phone-sex hotlines to see if they were real. (They were.)

What younger generations don’t understand—to their detriment—is that the immediacy and intimacy of the phone call produce different results from a text or direct message. Imagine asking your crush to prom and getting three dots on the screen over and over, your stomach doing backflips as you wait for a yes or no. The phone call is the communication version of getting punched in the face. Your knee-jerk reactions are on display. The ear on the other end of the receiver can sense emotions: anger, reluctance, joy, excitement. You feel all of it innately without FaceTime video.

As adults, there are several instances—even today, especially today—for which a phone call is superior to a text message. One of the greatest is simply catching up with an old friend. Seriously, when’s the last time you cold-called a buddy? Thirty minutes on the blower with one of your besties can be exhilarating. You cover far more ground with less wrist pain, and you can do it while driving or sitting as a passenger princess, putting your wired earbuds to good use. Yes, the shock of seeing your name appear might cause a moment of panic in your friend—Who died? But they’ll get over it fast, and quickly you’ll fall into an easy conversation. Don’t overthink it; just call someone.

In business, the phone call is even more crucial. If you can get someone on the phone, they almost have to give you an answer. Even if it isn’t the one you want to hear, you can move on with your life and stop sending follow-up emails every 48 hours. Efficiency is king, and the phone can help. After all, there’s a reason that in nearly every photo of a captain of industry he or she is walking and talking on a phone. They didn’t get to where they are through text alone. Plus, if you’re under 30, the person on the other end might even be impressed that you made the effort to pick up the phone and call. They’ll certainly remember you!

Of course, nostalgia plays a part, but I’m not suggesting you buy an old-school phone and hook it up to the nearest wall. Let’s focus on the practice; your iPhone has a calling function. Use it more frequently. Sometimes the newest thing isn’t the best. We have yet to improve on the phone call. Don’t be scared to call someone or to answer when it rings. Pick up and be happy someone is on the other side. That’s a blessing in its own right.

#6887. This Colorful South Carolina Home Marries Mediterranean Elegance With Lowcountry Charm

Designer Megan Molten worked handmade details and custom elements into every room.

How do you blend the magical ease of Mediterranean life with the coastal charm of South Carolina’s lowcountry? After an enchanting vacation in Greece with their three daughters, Megan Molten’s clients wanted their new build on Daniel Island to capture that unique, transportive feeling they fell in love with—and they asked the Charleston-based designer to help them achieve it.

Alongside builder Wes Whittle of EasTech Property Development, Molten was involved from the very beginning, crafting the blank canvas into a residence that balanced the sun-drenched elegance of the Greek islands with traditional Lowcountry living. The designer went all in on every detail, relying on high-quality craftsmanship, custom pieces, and handmade elements to bring life to every room.

Upon entry, guests are greeted by a hand-painted, botanical-themed mural by local artist Jenn Griffith. “The mural’s objective was very specific,” says Molten. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry.” Turn to the other side and find a plaster-covered stairwell by artist Emily Pope Harris, an addition that sets the tone for the whole home. Move further into the home and find a hand-painted floor that shines in the craft room, created using a stencil from Etsy and a few Sherwin-Williams paint colors—it’s playfully fabulous and still somehow timeless. “Clients do not typically splurge in rooms that are not front and center, so this was a real treat to bring to life, especially knowing how much their daughters love to craft,” the designer adds.

Elsewhere, a larger portion of the budget was split between the lighting and the kitchen. “Statement lighting in the house was always prioritized,” Molten says. “The homeowners also agreed that the lighting was important and needed to be special. I was finally able to use some light fixtures that I have been eyeing for years!”

As for the kitchen, it was more difficult to know where to splurge and where to save, a predicament the designer finds herself with on most projects. However, looking now at the honed limestone backsplash, green quartzite countertops with an integrated sink, and a plaster hood by Pope Harris, it’s clear that she made the right choices.

Even with all the home’s beautiful elements, the fan-favorite among Molten’s team ended up being the pool just outside the kitchen. It’s lower than most Molten has worked on in Charleston, making it one of the most unique she’s seen. Flanked by archways and paired with two spas and a raised hot tub, it fully captures a Grecian grotto feel.

Even with all the one-of-a-kind features and elevated furnishings, the home is a place where life is meant to be experienced to the fullest. “The spaces are to be enjoyed without hesitation,” Molten explains, “creating a beautiful, practical environment for a life well lived.”

FAST FACTSDesigner: Megan MoltenArchitect: DLB Custom Home DesignBuilder: EasTech Property DevelopmentLocation: Daniel Island, South CarolinaThe Space: A six-bedroom, seven-bathroom new build spanning 6,000 square feet.

The beauty of this entryway starts outside with the front door. Made locally by Maebeck Doors, it’s an arched shape with a grid pattern that serves as a charming “wow” moment upon entering the home.

Once inside the house, the awe continues, from the curved plaster stairwell to the custom botanical mural by artist Jenn Griffith. “The homeowners wanted a design that captured elements of both the Mediterranean and the Lowcountry,” Molten adds. The final touch? A stone table in the notch at the base of the stairs, perfect for placing keys or bags at the end of the day.

Molten’s client wanted a large, comfortable sectional that could easily seat her family and any guests, so when the team saw these two at High Point Market, they knew they were perfect for the space. A travertine table, nestled between the two sectionals, made the pieces feel like one long, continuous design, and both were upholstered in Crypton performance fabric for added durability.

To complement the space, Molten added a plush armchair and topped the room off with a modern light fixture that doesn’t add much noise. “The artwork above the sectional is from the homeowner’s own collection,” Molten says. “It served as the catalyst for the colorways used throughout the home.”

A calming, functional space, the brightness of the kitchen is achieved not through a classic all-white kitchen, but through shades of light green, from the quartzite countertops to the greenish-gray cabinets. Rattan pendant lights bring in that casual Mediterranean feel, especially since the floor-to-ceiling windows on the left overlook the pool below.

To keep it family-friendly, Molten applied one of her favorite tricks: leather on the counter stool seats and fabric on the back. “Wiping off messes and letting the leather patina is both practical and pretty,” she adds.

This dining nook really needed to be functional, since it’s where Molten’s clients and their three children sit for breakfast every morning and dinner every night. Durability for daily use played a huge part in the furniture choices; they sourced a long wooden table and used the same seating upholstery trick here as they did with the counter stools in the kitchen. For a touch of whimsy, Molten crowned the nook with a coco bead chandelier.

Instead of plaster on the double-sided fireplace as she had initially intended, Molten went with a zellige tile façade that produces a lovely shine.

Easily accessible from the living room, kitchen, and screened-in porch, this breakfast area—complete with a full bar—is perfect for hosting. Anyone can come in and make themselves a drink whenever they want.

Arched upper cabinets with lattice details create more movement in this space, as well as add in charm, and the chandelier has been on the designer’s list of favorites for a while. “I particularly love the contrast of the black countertop with the coral cabinets,” Molten says.

The first thing Molten selected for the scullery was the charming lattice window over the sink looking out onto the yard. Then came the bold wallpaper, which inspired the vibrant cabinet paint color, balanced out with a light Calacatta marble countertop with hints of yellow and gold throughout.

“This scullery is a very functional space that houses their microwave, coffee maker, refrigerator, and drawers with snacks, all built-in and panel-ready,” Molten says.

When her clients told her they wanted a dedicated craft room for their girls, Molten was just as excited at the prospect as their daughters. “A dedicated specialty room like this typically provides a great opportunity for lots of creativity and color,” she explains.

They started with the colorful striped wallpaper on the ceiling, pulling colors from it for the built-in cubby shelf and the painted floors. A neutral light fixture keeps the room from feeling too busy, giving it a more timeless feel. “The floor design is so special, and a bit of a splurge,” Molten adds. “I was so pleased the client embraced this idea!”

The unique wallpaper served as the starting point for this powder bath, as Molten designed everything else around it. “The Jamie Young mirror looks like an oyster shell, and the floating stone was a nice feature,” she says. “Painting the trim and ceiling the pink shell color made the wallpaper, tile, and stone come together.”

Molten had never done a wallpaper border before, but she adores how it turned out in one of the daughter’s bedrooms. “It’s a great way to add thoughtful design details to a room and is cost-effective too, as opposed to covering the full room in wallpaper,” she says. The trim color was pulled from this border, which she paired with a signature shade of pink from her own collection with Sherwin-Williams.

“For the daughter’s bathroom, we opted for a tub/shower combo to accommodate young children,” Molten explains. These children-friendly details are found elsewhere in the room too, like a pull-out step stool under the sink cabinets.

The pink-and-green palette from the daughter’s bedroom is continued here, though in slightly more saturated shades. A local Charleston brand, Mirth Studio, created the painted wood valance to pair with the Pepper Home shower curtain, and Pope Harris continued her plaster work here, covering a Visual Comfort & Co. sconce over the mirror in a custom pink hue.

Located on the small third floor, the only rooms that occupy this level are the office, the office den, and a guest suite, making it a quiet place to escape when the clients, who share this space, need to get work done. “Our client loved the Tiger Palm wallpaper, so we applied that on all of the walls and at the low part of the angled ceiling to help mask all of the ceiling lines in this room,” Molten says. They even have their own wet bar up here for post-work happy hour.

Designed as a dedicated no-kids zone off the office, this private room is where Molten’s clients get away to watch movies after bedtime, decompress after a long day, and just retreat to when they need time to themselves.

“We opted for a performance velvet on the sofa with velvet textured pillows to create a luxurious, cozy feel,” the designer says. “I love the Currey & Co. chandelier with green glass—it works so perfectly in this small room.”

“Like the primary bedroom and primary bathroom, the closet is not huge, but it’s efficient and thoughtfully designed,” Molten explains. With her team, they designed a wall of built-ins and paid close attention to the functionality of each drawer and shelf while maximizing the space; then a local cabinet maker brought them to life.

Molten chose soft, luxurious carpet that feels like stepping into a high-end department store and a fabulous Made Goods light fixture.

The architect who worked on this house, Damien Busillo of DLB Custom Home Design, had this lower-level pool incorporated in the original plans, and Molten was able to assist with the tile selections.

“This is such a creative way to use empty space under the house,” she says. “Homes in Charleston are often raised when built due to coastal flooding; however, this was the most interesting and exciting design concept I have seen for this area of the home.” It truly captures that Grecian grotto vibe.

Megan Molten has always been creative, but her love of interiors didn’t fully manifest until she bought and decorated her first house in Charleston, South Carolina. From there, she started experimenting with design as a hobby, sharing her eye with friends and family, before devoting all her time to it and finally deciding to open her own eponymous firm. Now, she shares her elevated coastal style and luxurious Lowcountry vibe with clients across the country.

#6886. Why Is It So Hard to Call Out Your Buddy?

Your friend does something terrible. You know you should say something, but you don’t. There’s a reason for that.

I joined three men for drinks recently, two of whom I am close with, one a casual acquaintance. A couple hours into the evening, once our french fries were soggy and the Modelos had done their work, the acquaintance told us about an argument he’d had with his girlfriend a few months back. It got physical, he said, leaning back in his chair. The fight ended with her in the hospital. He told the story like a man pleading his case before a jury, his eyes full of defiance and indignation but also fear. He rejected responsibility for her injuries and was desperate to be told he had done nothing wrong.

“There is just a lot of passion in our relationship,” he said. “If it was a big deal, she would have left me then.”

I watched him across the dark wooden table, my face as blank as I could manage. He looked to the other men as he spoke. I do not remember all of his excuses, only that they tumbled from his lips like a flood. I do remember that my two friends said nothing.

I know these two as good men. They are courageous and righteous in their hearts. Both would come running to stop an injustice. Neither abides a bully or petty authority. They have rescued adults from floods and picked up teenage daughters too afraid to drive home. But that night, faced with a buddy looking for forgiveness for something unforgivable over a beer, they could not muster a weak rebuke.

Later, I asked them why. Neither had a clear answer, both seeming troubled by their silence.

“If he had said all that about his dog instead of his girlfriend,” I told them, “I know you would have spoken up.”

They would have, they agreed. Immediately. Forcefully. Without hesitation.

These men do not value women less than dogs (though, in my opinion, valuing dogs very highly is a sign of good character). Yet they do not know why they missed this opportunity for bravery.

That decision is more significant than it seems in the moment. It is the kind of behavior that, at its extreme, can lead to situations such as the sexual-assault scandal at Cornell University. What makes that case so chilling is not just what Jane Doe alleges seven men did to her. It is that the story came with a glimpse into the fraternity’s culture through its group chats. There, we witnessed these men offer up Jane Doe like a party favor. And no one—not a single one of these promising young men at this Ivy League university who pledged this fraternity—had either the inclination or the courage to speak up against it.

This kind of behavior is a symptom of what is termed “precarious manhood” by social psychologists and researchers like me. And it’s harmful not only to women but to men too.

Precarious manhood does not mean that men themselves are fragile. Rather that their social status as a “real man” is.

Manhood is precarious in a way womanhood is not. Womanhood is seen as a natural process; it is biological. A girl develops physically and is deemed “a woman.” She may behave in myriad ways that are not considered feminine, but rarely is she declared “not a real woman.” Masculinity, on the other hand, is hard won yet easily lost. It is constructed and conditional. Men have to prove it over and over through acts and attitudes—get strong, fight back, run fast, dominate—not through biology.

As President Barack Obama said in 2021 on the Renegades podcast, “So much of popular culture tells [young men] that the only clear defining thing about being a man, being masculine, is you excel in sports and sexual conquest and violence.”

As boys learn early, the smallest transgression—cross your legs the wrong way, walk with the wrong gait, or cry in public—can lead to an accusation that you are “not a real man” or “girly.” To be weak and harmless is bad; to be womanlike is even worse. Even the insults used to humiliate men—you know the ones—are often descriptions that liken them to women.

When men try to prove that they fit into this standard of masculinity, other men are expected to acknowledge it. When you object to that performance, it is a challenge to the other man's behavior and, importantly, to his masculinity. That makes the objection more aggressive. It’s no longer just peer pressure trying to get you to go along with the group. Refusing to join in is a masculine threat. It signals that you are not fully aligned on the manly ideal, which means your own manhood can be questioned.

To measure the prevalence of precarious manhood, researchers have asked tens of thousands of men around the world to take a survey that, on a seven-point scale, asks how much they agree with statements like the following:

“The title of ‘manhood’ needs to be reserved for those who deserve it.”

“A male’s status as a real man sometimes depends on how other people view him.”

Countries that exhibit higher levels of precarious manhood are less happy, with lower life expectancy, gross domestic product, institutional trust, and social support. Men in these countries engage in riskier behavior and have higher rates of risk-related health problems, like cirrhosis of the liver and early death. Women, of course, also suffer. Heightened prevalence of precarious manhood predicts higher rates of violence against women, including sexual assault and domestic violence, as well as fewer legal protections for them.

The link between violence and misogyny can be seen clearly among those insecure in their manhood. Take the incel community as an extreme example. This dark corner of the manosphere is occupied by men who have trouble with women and blame them for their failures. Since these men are not sexually successful, and they see that as a challenge to their manhood, they ramp up their misogyny and violence. Incels have committed dozens of violent crimes, including mass shootings, to express their rage.

In 2025, I wrote about one self-described incel, who spent hours filming himself sexually harassing women in public and posting the videos online. Eventually, when the women rejected him, he pepper-sprayed them. The videos were wildly popular on incel discussion boards, and the perpetrator was considered a hero among them. His misogyny and violence helped increase his social standing.

Research has revealed how distressingly deep misogyny is entrenched in these perceptions of masculinity. In one study, men were confronted about either racist or sexist remarks. When they were challenged about racism, they tended to be apologetic. When they were confronted about sexism, they were amused. This tells us that, on average, these men see misogyny as not worth correcting.

When women confronted men about sexist comments, the men tended to think the women were overreacting. When they were confronted by other men, however, they took the corrections more seriously. Because participants tended to be generally less concerned about sexist remarks, the researchers mused that men may be less likely to intervene, believing it wouldn’t make a difference. That, in turn, creates a culture of passive acceptance.

If men have the strength to stand up, though, research also shows that it makes a difference. When people are confronted about biased statements, they are more likely to change their behavior going forward. Further research has shown that group norms can influence and explain men’s misogynistic behavior. When men are with other men who are less tolerant of sexism, even if they have just met, the group is less likely to use sexist behavior as a way to prove their masculinity. But when men are in a group where sexist behavior is common, they are more likely to sexually objectify and disrespect women. They do this explicitly to gain social status—exactly as precarious manhood would suggest. These same men are also less likely to step in and help a woman who is in a sexually uncomfortable situation, like what we’ve seen with the Cornell case.

There is a better model for masculinity. “Expansive masculinity” describes a vision of manhood that values flexibility as a strength, leadership through connection and empathy, and the bravery in vulnerability. This model rejects that masculinity must be earned through dominance or proved constantly. It allows men to define themselves by their values and relationships rather than by their conformity to a narrow standard.

Such a model shifts masculinity from something that is externally validated to something embodied in personal integrity. It finds strength not in stoicism and domination but in the capacity for empathy, understanding, and connection with others. When other men behave badly, expansive masculinity calls for men to object, to stand up for their personal values, and to protect their communities.

My two friends have taken steps toward expansive masculinity. One of them was approached by a colleague who was accused of sexually harassing women at work. Like our acquaintance at the bar, that colleague was brimming with excuses and confrontation, looking for absolution. He wanted my friend to tell him it was okay or to tell him the women overreacted.

Instead, my friend told him, “You have made serious mistakes in the way you treat young women.” The colleague argued, but my friend held firm. “It’s more than one incident,” he said. “And just one is unacceptable.”

Meanwhile, my other friend came up with a simple practice: Whenever another man behaves out of line, make it muscle memory to say, “What the fuck?”

When bad behavior spreads in barrooms and chat rooms and locker rooms, it infects boys and young men. Promoting a healthier side of masculinity, one that sees care, protection, empathy, and vulnerability as strong character traits and misogyny as unacceptable, offers an alternative path. That leadership, strength of conviction, and commitment to others is ultimately what makes someone a good man.

#6883. Everyone Wants to Know if the MacBook Neo Will Go on Sale for Black Friday

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

The cheapest MacBook might get even cheaper.

I am not the first person to point out that MacBooks are expensive. I will not be the last. If you monitor this stuff like we do, you’ll have seen the irony that Apple released the budget-conscious MacBook Neo earlier this year—only for the global computing shortage to almost immediately raise the price from $599 to $689.

But, the Neo is still cheap. You can buy it full-price and get your money’s worth. That said, we’d take the bet that it will go on sale. A handful of Prime Days have come and gone without any discounts to the newest MacBook, but with the new flagship released, we suspect that it Black Friday might give us a Christmas miracle.

Designed as Apple’s answer to the lower budget laptops running Windows, the Neo’s target audience is technically students—hence the good branding and cute colors. That being said, if you need a personal computer for watching Netflix and doing your taxes, but don’t want a full desktop, it’s a perfect choice.

The Neo runs on Apple’s A18 Pro chip—the same one that powers the iPhone 16 Pro and Pro Max. If the idea of buying a laptop that runs on a phone chip gives you pause, your stress is better channeled elsewhere. While this isn’t the right device for someone who is planning to run heavy-duty software, it can handle standard internet browsing, video calls, and the like with ease. Just keep in mind that it only has 8GB of RAM, so it’s best-suited to basic workflows.

Of course, this computer has the added benefit of four jazzy color options: blush and citrus for the bold, indigo for those more subdued, and silver for that classic MacBook aesthetic. Wrapped in an aluminum body with a 13-inch Liquid Retina display, the Neo weighs in at just 2.7 pounds (same as the MacBook Air) and is 0.5 inches thick. (For reference, the iPhone 17 Pro Max is 0.35 inches thick.)

Despite our best efforts, we don’t quite know how Apple determines its deals for big sale events—which means if this laptop does indeed get discounted, there’s no telling exactly how deep the price cut might be or when it’ll hit. Maybe it’ll shave $100 off to get back to its pre-increase price, or maybe we could be looking at a sub-$500 MacBook for the first time ever.

Whatever the discount may be, the Neo is undoubtedly a smart buy for anyone who wants a laptop that can handle all of the basics. Even at full price, it’s a fraction of the cost of a discounted MacBook Pro or Air, which reliably go on sale for Black Friday.

If you’re not willing to wait for a potential Neo deal, a handful of other MacBooks are already on sale.

#6880. I Have No Intention of Celebrating the Blue Tsunami Until Scott Colom Is Officially Elected

#6878. Cole Hauser Is Going Undercover for the FBI. (Sort of.)

The Dutton Ranch actor will star in a new series about a real-life FBI agent who infiltrated neo-Nazi groups.

Cole Hauser went toe to toe with Texas as Dutton Ranch’s Rip Wheeler. Now, he’ll take on white supremacist groups, drug traffickers, and motorcycle gangs in a new series based on former undercover FBI agent Scott Payne’s 2025 memoir, Code Name: Pale Horse: How I Went Undercover to Expose America's Nazis.

According to Deadline, the Yellowstone actor is developing the project with members of Constantin Film (Resident Evil), with an aim to star in the lead role as well. The story would follow Payne, or a character much like him, who spent two decades working undercover in extremist groups to bring them to justice from the inside.

One scene from Payne’s memoir includes a Halloween night in which he had to slaughter and drink the blood of a goat with a violent neo-Nazi group in Georgia. He’s burned crosses with the Ku Klux Klan and smuggled drugs across the border—all to bring in hundreds of arrests for the FBI.

“Feeling so blessed! Let’s go!!!!” Payne reacted to the news on social media.

Dubbed “The Hillbilly Donnie Brasco,” Payne’s memoir also details the struggles of undercover work, as well as how he managed his marriage, his faith, and fatherhood throughout some of the most dangerous operations in FBI history.

“These are the type of stories that I am drawn to as a producer and as an actor,” Hauser told Deadline. “Scott Payne is a real-life hero, with the kind of moxie and edge you would need to risk your life to fight evil. I am looking forward to producing this movie and getting a chance to bring him and his story to life on the screen.”

Elsewhere, Hauser is also working on a series about the origins of modern-day poker with Sylvester Stallone titled Blood Aces: The Wild Ride of Benny Binion. He told Esquire in 2023 that he was in the early stages of a series where he and Matthew McConaughey would play brothers, written by True Detective’s Nick Pizzolatto. (Brothers may have gotten in the way of that one.) But Hauser’s also gearing up for the release of Dutton Ranch season 2, which will likely air sometime in 2027.

#6876. James Gunn’s DC Universe Was Weird, Ambitious, and Ultimately Unfinished

The writer-director is a casualty of Skydance’s acquisition of Warner Bros. He was building to a future that fans will now likely never see, for better or worse.

The hierarchy of power at DC Studios is changing again. On November 9, news broke that writer-director James Gunn and his producing partner Peter Safran left—later reported as ousted from—their jobs as co-CEOs and co-chairmen of DC Studios. Jeremy Latcham, a former Marvel Studios producer whose past credits include The Avengers and Gunn’s own Guardians of the Galaxy, will replace them on December 1.

Suddenly, DC fans woke up on a Monday to a new regime. But how will we look back on the four years that a prolific genre writer and director, who came up under Lloyd Kaufman’s B-movie studio Troma, oversaw an entire superhero universe?

It was a time of a lot of promise, not all of it fulfilled.

Before we start the eulogies for the DC Universe, it’s important to note that things aren’t over. Yet. Latcham will take over Gunn and Safran’s posts, so DC Studios is still up and running with more movies in the pipeline, many of which bear Gunn’s fingerprints. Man of Tomorrow, for example. The 2027 movie is a direct sequel to Superman, also written and directed by Gunn. Among its biggest moves is introducing a new Wonder Woman (rumored, but not confirmed, to be Adria Arjona). The Batman: Part II, the long-awaited sequel to Matt Reeves’s The Batman, will also open in 2028 with Gunn and Safran involved as producers. Gunn also said on Instagram they have been “engaged on another DC project which I’m writing.”

Both Gunn and Safran are expected to stay at DC, with Deadline confirming they will “continue to produce on various DC projects.” That said, Matthew Belloni at Puck published a story citing sources who say the DCU will reboot, again, after Man of Tomorrow. And at The Hollywood Reporter, Borys Kit and Aaron Crouch detailed the friction Gunn faced while at the studio, and how Gunn and Safran’s exits were forced by new Skydance leaders David Ellison and Ynon Kreiz. THR reports the DC heads felt secure after the big Warner-Paramount merger; Clayface had grossed $100 million, and Gunn was preparing a draft for the third Superman movie when they were told they were out. You can say the DCU is in a crisis right now.

For now, we can say that a critical chapter of DC is over. Gunn will no longer apply his vision and filter across all DC projects, which he must feel a little relieved about. By his own admission, which he wrote on Instagram on November 9, Gunn expressed feeling stretched thin, having to exert energy on movies and shows he wasn’t emotionally invested in to begin with. Said Gunn: “[T]he thing my ADHD hyperfocus has had the most difficulty with has been dividing my time between projects I’m incredibly passionate about and the ones I’m not so much and all the things I have to do as a studio head.”

Gunn and Safran got their jobs in late 2022, but it wasn’t until 2024 that people saw the first glimpse of their reign. Though Gunn had already worked for DC on 2021’s The Suicide Squad and created the spin-off Peacemaker, those projects were separate from other things at DC, then run by Walter Hamada. At the time, its biggest bet was Black Adam, Dwayne Johnson’s long-in-development antihero project that fell flat and led to nowhere. When Gunn took the reins of DC soon after, the former Marvel director had carte blanche to tailor the sandbox to his vision.

In January 2023, Gunn revealed his plans for DC Studios in something he called “Chapter 1: Gods and Monsters.” In a six-minute YouTube video, Gunn ran down his plans for the movies and shows that made up his master plan for the next decade of DC Studios. But for every mention of this movie or that HBO Max show, the first project carried significant weight. It was the first step, the real first impression, as a showcase of what Gunn would do with a DCU bent to his will. What icons would make it to the screen? What legend would get their spotlight? What iconic storylines might be retold on the big screen? What, really, will James Gunn’s DC become?

His first order of business was... Creature Commandos.

In fairness, Creature Commandos was a great show (if you saw it). It was an adult animated series where horror and pulp-inspired superheroes teamed up as a black ops strike force. While a show like that wouldn’t be anyone’s idea of a great introduction, Gunn’s background as the writer of movies like Tromeo & Juliet and his success with Guardians of the Galaxy for Marvel at least made sense. It basically said without ambiguity that this new DCU is being molded by no one else but Gunn.

Then, Superman premiered in summer 2025. Initially written by Gunn, the DC Studios head eventually named himself the director of DC’s first theatrical release under his stewardship. Despite my own misgivings about the movie, it was the reorientation a refreshed superhero universe needed. You simply can’t have a DC Universe without the Man of Steel, whose first appearance in Action Comics #1 in 1938 kicked off the modern superhero as we know it.

Superman made a solid first impression for DC at the cineplex. So there was plenty of optimism for the 2026 spin-off Supergirl starring Milly Alcock. Largely based on the acclaimed Tom King and Bilquis Evely graphic novel Supergirl: Woman of Tomorrow, the pseudo-sequel was poised to keep up DC’s newfound momentum. It didn’t. Supergirl hit a wall, much like Black Adam. No superhero universe is immune to duds, but given it was just DC’s second time at bat, a blemish on what still felt like a new, fresh-out-of-the-box DCU felt grave.

But one dud wasn’t enough to kneecap the DCU. The same summer saw the premiere of Lanterns, which fostered a faithful audience on HBO Sunday nights during its buzzy eight-episode first season. Despite mixed reviews, Clayface has grossed a profitable $100 million against a slim budget. Between the next Superman and Batman sequels, there was still plenty to look forward to. Until now.

James Gunn’s reign wasn’t impervious to bad news either. Since his January 2023 reveal, many of the projects have wound up on the back burner: The Authority, which had been a “passion project” for Gunn (with plenty of setup for it in Superman); Waller, a TV show with Viola Davis reprising her role from the Suicide Squad films; Paradise Lost, a political epic set in Wonder Woman’s home; and Booster Gold, a superhero comedy about a goofball from the future. And throughout his reign, Gunn put up with questions about his Batman (not Matt Reeves’s), which he would have introduced in The Brave and the Bold. Gunn sweated over the character so much that a cameo of Batman in Creature Commandos was shrouded in shadow, because he said he was still deciding on what the costume was going to look like.

Since the start of Gunn’s DC, it was clear his architecture came from a place of affection for the brand, and for very specific corners of it. His body of work reflected his scraggly upbringing as a punk creative, with empathy for characters who were too weird for their own Saturday morning cartoon. The Suicide Squad elevated such figures, like Weasel and Polka-Dot Man, whose climax involved a fight with Starro, a colossal evil starfish. Though his Superman was a sunny throwback to the Christopher Reeve classics, it was also jam-packed with the likes of Green Lantern, Mister Terrific, Metamorpho, and a villain Gunn was clearly setting up to be Bizarro.

Multiple times on social media, Gunn shared artwork from one DC book in particular: Kingdom Come, written by Mark Waid and illustrated by Alex Ross. Published in 1996, the story was a treatise of sorts by Waid, who lamented the state of superhero comics at the time. The story sees an older Superman who comes out of retirement to reform the Justice League, only to meet physical and philosophical resistance—predominantly from Batman.

Gunn’s allusions to Kingdom Come, including the exact shape of Superman’s “S” in his film, suggested he was laying the groundwork for a Kingdom Come-style event movie. It would have been an ambitious endeavor, oddly fitting to Gunn’s sensibilities. In the few years we saw Gunn at work for DC, he was building a universe where tried-and-true icons of superhero comics brushed up against the prickly and the bizarre. Where weirdness leaked from the orifices of a summer tentpole with an action figure set.

It often feels like Gunn was just getting started, but four years with two (soon three) movies written and directed by him, several TV shows—and Supergirl and Clayface, whatever you think of them—leave a big enough impression to suggest that Gunn’s DC had no shortage of imagination, even if his signature flourishes had grown familiar. Not every superhero film needs to have shock F-bombs and action scenes set to ironic licensed music, but Gunn had a lane. He excelled at staying in it.

It’s doubtful Gunn and Safran (who took on the business end of things) will stay on at DC under Skydance leadership for long. They will probably see Man of Tomorrow and The Batman: Part II through. If those movies do well, they might stick around to keep making movies, because for the most part, they have a magic that works. If the new movies fail to take off, then Gunn might finally find new stories to tell. It’s hard to say which path is the better one.

Gunn’s Kingdom Come, whatever it might have been, pointed to a future that never became a promise. In his farewell message, Gunn wrote that “the characters we set up will live on.” Whether the next era will let the DCU stay weird is another matter.
