#6874. The 12 Best Amber Colognes to Buy Right Now

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

These are the season’s top cold-weather compliment getters.

It has been an unusually warm fall so far, but today, at least in New York City, there’s finally a chill in the air. I got so excited when I saw the temperature was in the low 50s this morning that I may have rushed the season a tad and put on a thick sweater under an insulated jacket. So, I was sweating in the subway, but at least I smelled good—because when it comes to cold-weather fragrance, I know what I’m doing.

If you’re looking to add to your winter cologne rotation, there’s no better place to start than a good amber scent. When you think of fragrance as an accessory, it becomes something you strive to match with your outfit, and amber colognes pair perfectly with corduroy, thick coats, and soft scarves. But what exactly does amber smell like? After all, the kind of amber that probably comes to mind—fossilized tree amber—doesn’t actually have much of a scent.

Well, when we say “amber” in the fragrance world, it really refers to a blend of natural resins and synthetics that combine to produce a warm, rich, and sweet smell. Some classic scent notes that make up the amber fragrance family include labdanum (a resinous extract from a Mediterranean shrub), benzoin (a sweet, balsamic tree resin), ambroxan (a synthetic fragrance molecule with a warm, woody aroma), vanilla, and tonka bean.

Here, we’ve pulled together our definitive list of the top amber scents for men, covering every vibe, lifestyle, and scent story you’re trying to tell. Whether you’re leaning into smoky sophistication, something on the sweeter side, or going for “I naturally smell this good,” there’s something here for everyone. And with the holidays fast approaching, consider these foolproof gift options for anyone on your list who appreciates smelling like they have their life together.

Inspired by the luxury fashion label’s most iconic men’s silhouette, this fragrance is designed to capture the essence of a tuxedo, using notes that convey sophistication and sharp black-tie tailoring into scent. Grounded in earthy patchouli and layered with subtle spice, it radiates a refined, confident energy that’s perfect for winter yet versatile enough for wearing it year-round.

The purpose of Maison Margiela’s Replica line is that the scents are made to capture memories and moods, the scent of a specific moment. By the Fireplace is easily one of the most evocative scents in the collection—my personal favorite, for what it’s worth—because smoke and warm chestnut instantly transport you somewhere near a warm crackling fire.

It starts with a bit of spice and smoke, and once it settles on the skin, that initial smokiness softens into a smooth, vanilla warmth. The rest of the fragrance leans very heavily into the sweetness of amber. It’s a compliment-getter, if that’s what you’re after.

If you want to channel your inner Alexander Skarsgård in Pillion (IYKYK) with a scent that radiates rugged confidence, subtle earthiness, and that intoxicating new-leather smell that only comes from a new pair of boots or a leather trench, look no further. Malin + Goetz’s Leather Eau de Parfum has been a staple in my collection for years

It’s leathery and musky, but it has a good hit of cedar that combines to leans it into the world of amber. It also has a lot of floral notes as it dries on the skin to balance it all out. It’s a nicely layered fragrance. A purist might not call this amber, but we think it’s well within the sweet, resinous world of these other fragrances.

Not ready to commit to the full bottle? The unisex cologne also comes as a perfume oil in a compact rollerball perfect for a trial run or as a stocking stuffer.

As I noted in my intro, amber fragrances can take on a variety of scent profiles, all capturing the warmth and depth of true amber. Rich gourmand options like Horace’s Vintage Vanilla are a great pick if you love scents inspired by the culinary world, but don’t worry, it’s expertly balanced with a mix of woodiness and spice so you won’t end up smelling like you went overboard on your marshmallow-y Victoria’s Secret body mist.

Don’t even want to smell like an edible vanilla confection? No problem. The brand’s signature Oud Rose is another solid amber-forward scent that leans on floral notes to balance its intensity.

Diptyque is another one of those bougie fragrance houses everyone recognizes, yet for some reason I rarely reach for their scents over my tried-and-true favorites. But after smelling L’Eau Trois Eau de Toilette, I might have to rethink that stance. This one immediately transported me to being in Italy and stepping into a Duomo, where incense and myrrh linger in the air.

It takes a surprisingly verdant and green approach to the amber family, but those resinous top notes anchor everything with a spicy-smoky warmth. The result is a scent that feels refined, atmospheric, and just mysterious enough to keep you coming back for another sniff.

Sure, amber scents can be sweet and gourmand, but they can also be spicy and sophisticated. Giardini di Toscana’s Bianco Oro is a great example of the latter. At first spritz, you mostly get spicy pink and black pepper with a backdrop of amber, but as the scent lingers, the rich amber notes take center stage along with smooth musks and creamy woods. As it fades, it becomes a bit powdery, but the trio of spices, amber, and musks are still there. It’s the perfect scent to wear out to a nice dinner or evening event, but it works just as well in the daytime.

It’s a general rule that amber and woods go great together, since amber provides a warm richness that blends well with dry, crisp woods. That combination is at the foundation of Dolce & Gabbana’s The One for Men EDP, but before we get there, we actually get a touch of bright and juicy citrus from Tarocco orange and grapefruit as well as a natural, herbal hint of clary sage at the heart that adds a bit of earthiness to this fragrance. Amber and woods mingle at the base, grounding the lighter, more vibrant opening in something warm and rich.

Guerlain makes a handful of luxury amber fragrances, but Tobacco Honey is the one that stands out from the rest. It’s an intense blend of—you guessed it—tobacco leaves and golden honey, which creates a beautifully bold contrast of smoky and sweet. It emphasizes the sweet side of the ambery tobacco accord at the heart of this scent, enveloping the dry tobacco notes in vanilla, tonka bean, and sesame to create a rich, creamy heart that gets lifted up by vibrant, sweet honey. If you’ve ever wanted to try a tobacco fragrance but worried it would smell too smoky (or too much like cigarettes), this is the one that will change your mind.

For something a bit lighter and more airy than the mostly rich and deep amber scents on this list, try Golden Morning from Lafco’s new (and first-ever) personal fragrance collection. Like a cup of hot chamomile tea with honey, it’s soothing, warm, and a little sweet. Bright and citrusy at the top with bergamot and apple blossom, it then introduces chamomile and a floral heart of lavender, violet leaf, and rose. The dry down is smooth and sweet with honey, tonka bean, amber, and musks.

Okay, I’m not saying the other fragrances on this list can’t also be considered “bougie,” but Maison Francis Kurkdjian Paris’ Baccarat Rouge 540 takes luxury to an entirely new level. And yes, while the price tag is particularly eye-watering, I have to admit: this scent is absolutely worth it. A perfectly balanced blend of woody, floral, and ambery notes, this fragrance uses high-quality ingredients with a higher molecular weight, meaning they cling to the skin longer and create a richer scent profile with impressive staying power. In short, a little goes a very long way with this one…if that helps you justify the price tag.

Sitting somewhere between gourmand and citrus on the fragrance spectrum, Arquiste’s L’Or de Louis is one of those amber scents that just radiates richness. The 24K gold flakes are a little too on-the-nose, but the the story behind the fragrance does a better job of telling a story of opulence. It was inspired by the moments when King Louis XIV and his court strolled Versailles’ Grand Orangery, enjoying the flowering orange trees warmed by bonfires.

It also won The Fragrance Foundation’s Indie Fragrance of the Year Award in 2024, so the hoity-toity storytelling is backed by a scent that actually smells…well…good. The result is a golden aura of perfectly blended smokiness, fruitiness, and depth. A fragrance truly fit for a king.

If your nose is on the more sensitive side and you’re looking for a warm, enveloping scent that doesn’t overwhelm with heavy muskiness, D’Orsay’s Te dire oui. V.H. Eau de Parfum is a perfect choice. Built on leather, it avoids the overpowering muskiness found in many amber and leather fragrances, opting instead for a cleaner, sportier interpretation rounded out by a subtle saffron and rose finish.

This aligns perfectly with the brand’s philosophy, which favors fragrances that act as personal rituals—subtle companions that are seamlessly woven into everyday life—rather than scents that dominate every room you enter.

#6872. The 6 Best Smart Cameras for Building Out Your Home Security

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

You can’t buy total peace of mind, but you can get pretty close.

The open doors era of American life is well and truly behind us. Instead, we have apps on our phones that notify us when a package gets delivered (or stolen) or when your kid gets home. You can have these things set up to be as jumpy as you are, detecting every little unknown motion in the driveway. Sorry, just a chipmunk.

In reality, nothing will give you total peace, but you can come pretty damn close with the help of a smart security camera. It doesn’t even take a full ADP system anymore. All you need is a one-time camera purchase, maybe a cheap subscription, and a phone—which you already have. At the very least, it's a whole lot better than leaving your front light on to deter intrusions while you're away for the week.

If you're in the market for a new security camera or video doorbell, you've come to the right place. Any of these options will keep you, your loved ones, and (most importantly) your treasured, and very expensive, belongings safe. From doorbell cams to floodlight cams to wild ones that you stick on your window, these are the six best (and smartest) home security cameras out there.

When I'm setting up an outdoor security camera, there are only three things I care about: Durability, battery life, and storage. Everything else—like smart recognition, video quality, and notifications—doesn’t matter as much. Give me 1080p resolution, an app that crashes all the time, and no advanced AI, and honestly, I really won’t mind. Thankfully, this camera is a bit better than that with an app that, for the record, does not crash all the time.

Arlo's Pro wireless camera is great in all three areas. First, it's properly weather resistant. It can handle storms, heat, and cold without any dips in performance. Second, without an Arlo Smart subscription ($3 a month for unlimited video storage) you can store one-week's worth of video on the cloud, which gives you more than enough time to download videos of particular events of interest. I wish there was some free local storage (even a couple gigs is enough to cycle recent footage), but at least the fee is reasonable.

It’s solid on the battery front too, with rechargeable batteries that only need to be swapped out about once every month. If you want to pay $199, you can get the version of the camera with an extra large battery, just note it is a bit bulkier.

Budget options don't get better than the Wyze cam. It plays nice with Google Assistant and Alexa, and the newest version boasts improved image quality and better night vision. This 2.5K resolution camera technically has a higher resolution than the Arlo Pro, which is nearly three times the price. What you’re paying for there is a wireless connection. This camera is entirely wired, needing to be plugged in to operate. As a result, the Wyze cam continues to be an excellent option for indoor security, but not one I’d recommend for outdoor usage.

You can pay Wyze for cloud storage, but it’s not the only option this camera gives. I love that Wyze allows users to bring their own MicroSD cards and use them for local storage. Chances are you have one somewhere, and these days ones with massive amounts of storage come relatively cheap. That truly makes this the budget smart camera option for indoor security.

If you want a nice-looking high-performance video doorbell, the new Nest is pretty much the full package. It always sends a crisp pitch right to your phone, and the camera itself has a wide enough field of view that you never feel like you’re missing something just out of frame. And if wired isn’t your speed, you can also grab a battery-powered version.

While I always appreciate a free cloud storage option, Google’s is pathetic. I can’t rightfully say it’s bad on purpose, but after a week of only being able to save 10 second clips you’ll be begging to give your $10 a month for Google Home Premium. This also unlocks advanced AI face detection features that make the base experience feel that much worse.

For apartments, townhomes, or anyone who already has a peephole, this is the perfect doorbell camera. Just pop out your current peephole and slide this one in. It’s really simple as that, no bullshit.

The only issue, as with any wireless camera, is battery life. Since this one is guaranteed to get a lot of traffic in front of it, battery changes are going to be pretty frequent, about every three weeks. That said, it's not a huge deal. The camera comes with two rechargeable battery packs, so you just have to make sure the spare is always charged and ready to go. At some point in the three week period, you’re bound to find the time.

The unfortunate downside of going with Ring is the subscription fee. There’s no free cloud storage so you’re going to be writing (another) annual check to Amazon if you want to use this one.

If you need a camera and floodlight combo, you have tons of options from every brand that makes these things. Any worthwhile one is going to be wired, that’s just how it is. Most of those aren’t bad, but Ring’s is especially good. It’s a wide-angle 2K video, with a 6X zoom, more than enough to get important details when you need them. The lights themselves are bright and sensitive (although not overly sensitive because that’s straight up annoying). The rest of it works as you’d expect, with all the controls and custom features the Ring app offers at your disposal.

The storage is both the best and worst thing about this camera. While I’ve complained about the lack of free storage on Ring devices already in this article, your $5 a month does go a long way. For some people that will be better than having to deal with local storage solutions. Still, I wish Ring offered the option.

So you want something you can move around whenever—maybe from the nursery to the backyard and back three times a week? For you, Eufy makes an adaptable window camera that mounts to your window using suction. Once you have it secured, you can use the knob to easily adjust the rotation. And, when it’s time to move it all you do is remove the Velcro mount and place it on the desired window on the other side of the house.

For something like a floodlight camera, I don’t find the wired setup to be a huge imposition for many people. With something like this window camera, being tethered via USB cord limits where you can place it. Depending on your home, this may or may not end up being an issue you need to account for.

What Do We Look for in Home Security Cameras?

Really, it boils down to a few things. Most importantly, in my mind, are durability, power, and video storage, but things like video quality, smart home compatibility, and motion detection also play a role.

Durability - How weather resistant is the camera? Here, all the cameras shown are IP65 or higher, meaning they're just about waterproof. I wouldn't go for any less.

Power - I'd always recommend going wired here. Wireless, especially for high-up security cameras, can be a bit of a pain to keep charged. Though, a lot of people prefer the ease of setup for wireless.

Video storage - Want unlimited cloud storage? It'll cost you. However, most cameras have some amount of complimentary storage. I wouldn't recommend anything under a week's worth of digital storage. And even then, I'd prefer a local option, as it's cheaper and easier to deal with.

Video quality - 1080p on a doorbell is fine. 2K on a security camera gets the job done. 2.5k or 3K video is where you want to be. 4k video is nice to have, but honestly it’s a bit overkill.

Motion detection - Most cameras are just OK at this. Brands claim their cameras are smart and can tell the difference between you and the UPS guy, but often that's a lie. At this price point, you just want something that can tell the difference between human and car. After that, it just needs to reliably detect motion and capture it.

#6870. House Republicans Couldn’t Have Picked a Worse Minority Speaker

So much for cleaning house. Rep. Jim Jordan of Ohio has been a loyal foot soldier of the MAGA regime for years now.

Politico has a very strange story about the battle for Republican leadership in the House of Representatives. Speaker Mike Johnson already has scratched himself from the race to become the next minority leader. And here is Politico’s take, which seems very, very odd.

So far, this is a run-of-the-mill state of the losers’ postelection story. The losing side, particularly a catastrophically losing side, always spends some time in public introspection. It’s almost a kind of penance. But here’s where the story spins out.

As I’m sure Stutzman knows, on any issue that both involves the administration and drove voters in the midterm elections, there’s not a dime’s worth of difference between Scalise and Jordan (R-Van Heusen). They have both been loyal foot soldiers of the MAGA regime. Both of them supported at least one objection to certify the results in 2020. On Tuesday, Jordan thumped Scalise and became the GOP leader, 106–73, and it will be interpreted as another defeat for Johnson’s old leadership team. But the promotion of Jordan doesn’t answer any of the problems that contributed to the loss of Congress by the Republicans. Jordan’s knees haven’t stopped jerking since 2017. He hunted every snipe at which the administration pointed, even the ones that didn’t exist, which was a substantial number of them. He was rude and approximately as committed to bipartisanship as a manhole cover. If the primary issue in the election was corruption, Jordan has made a career out of supplying alibis.

I hope Speaker-in-Waiting Jeffries realizes all of this. This is the legislative equivalent of Stephen Miller running the White House. I’d say the Republicans could have found someone more dedicated to getting things done, but there isn’t anybody like that left.

#6868. It’s Always Daiquiri Season

The simple, rum-based cocktail has been compromised by identity theft. So what is it, really?

The daiquiri has been compromised by identity theft. Down in Louisiana, the New Orleans Original Daiquiris chain has sullied the classic cocktail’s good name by slinging slushy sugar bombs with outrageous names like the Banana Banshee, Mudslide, and Crawgator.

Savvy Esquire readers know that the original drink involves nothing more than rum, lime juice, and sweetener. Yet modern specialists like Vay Su, cofounder of Vandell in Los Angeles, can take you on a Caribbean odyssey by ruminating on the minutiae: What kind of limes, and how fresh? Cane sugar or simple syrup? Shaved ice or a fat chunk? And how much of the rum character should force its way through?

Restaurants like Kabawa in New York’s East Village have transformed the humble daiquiri into an unforgettable drinking experience. When you step in and spy other people enjoying a concoction poured over 70 heaping grams of shaved ice, you already know what you’re going to order. Kathryn “Pepper” Stashek, Kabawa’s creative bartender—yes, that’s really her title—never gets tired of seeing that look in everyone’s eyes that says, Obviously, I need a daiquiri.

At Kabawa, you get to choose your rum too. Opt for the Jamaica–Barbados blend of Probitas or go agricole with Montebello Rhum Blanc from the islands of Guadeloupe. Maybe veer down a vintage alley with Bacardi Carta Blanca from 1978. You can’t go wrong; the only mistake is assuming you need to wait for warm weather. As Su says, it’s always daiquiri season.

Want to whip up a classic daiquiri at home? Here’s how to do it the Esquire way.

#6867. You Don’t Need a Smartwatch. You Need This $55 Timex.

The Ironman is a no-fuss watch that’ll help you get in shape without overcomplicating it.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Welcome to the Esquire Endorsement. Heavily researched. Thoroughly vetted. These picks are the best way to spend your hard-earned cash.

I’m not trying to run a marathon here. I just want to feel strong, feel healthy in my normal life. In fact, a flight of stairs was my tipping point. It was midsummer. I hit a jog up the stairs—for fun, just to move—coming out of my usual subway stop below the Esquire offices in Manhattan. Two long flights, maybe 50 steps total. I got to the top, step onto the street, and ... what the hell. I was gassed. Hands on my knees on a public sidewalk, gassed. I desperately needed to get back in the gym.

Let me clarify before friends and family text me to call bullshit, I had not let myself go; I was just a little out of shape. No one could tell, except for my older brother—he has been my tiger mom since I was born—who made sure to tell me after he invited my wife and me out for a Fire Island beach day. He could tell, and my lungs could tell—that’s what matters. Prior to that 30-second jog, I’d tried all the smart fitness trackers that are meant to game you into working out. None stuck. Life was too busy, my willpower was too weak. I wasn’t hitting the gym.

Then I bought a Timex Ironman. My thought, a stroke of genius in hindsight, was to have it within reach as much as possible. My workouts got idiot-proof, simple beyond belief: Manchester United’s 20-minute sprint test, kettlebell circuits, push-up and pull-up splits, or some form of lower-body lifting. Am I in the best shape of my life? No. But I could run a 5K right now, no problem. I’m back to basics and in good-enough shape. That’s all because of this watch.

If you are one of those high-achieving men that feels the need to run marathons, hike ultras, or cycle hundreds of miles, your qualm with a Timex Ironman won’t be the durability. “Ironman” does, in fact, come from the idea that this would be your companion over 140 miles of swim-bike-run. It’s water-resistant to 100 meters, plenty for swimming laps. It has a chronograph, alarm, and timer—the only things you actually need to track a workout. Want to know if your VO2 max is getting better? Or your lifts are paying off? Track numbers: Splits get faster, reps get higher. You don’t need all the heart-rate stuff.

And, crucially, there isn’t much to break down on the inside.

But suppose your Timex Ironman does break down. Unless you got an anomaly or wear it into literal war, this shouldn’t happen for about a decade; my dad has owned one Ironman since before I was born in the 90s. Even if you only hit five to seven years, that’s longer than a smartwatch lasts before getting phased out because of software improvements. That’s a $500 replacement. An Ironman is a tenth of that.

It’s also worth saying that I do not have an issue with smartwatches or fitness trackers; they are truly revolutionary when it comes to catching hidden health problems. And if it’s your jam to track steps, count calories, and see little rings that tell you about VO2 max and sleep scores—by all means, do what works for you. But that line of thinking just does not work on me. A screen on my wrist means I’m constantly fiddling with my playlist or thinking about the text messages and emails just on the other side of Do Not Disturb. None of that is what I need when working out.

Since switching over to my Ironman, I’ve found myself not even having my phone on me during a workout. I’ve gone full Luddite—something I always laughed at in the gym but now have huge respect for. Everything becomes harder without a workout playlist, but it also becomes more zen. On a run, you listen to your feet on the pavement. During a lift, you listen to your breathing. You get a lot more in tune with your body.

This, however, is the real crux of my argument. The little smartwatch tracking rings don’t do it for me, and nothing pisses me off more than a notification badge. It’s a nerd with a stat sheet saying, “Well, if you observe last night’s sleep score and this morning’s use of caffeine, you’ll see it’s a great time to get in the gym,” while I’m sitting at the beach reading a book on vacation. I don’t want to be told when to work out or how hard to workout by someone’s algorithm.

What I want is something that sparks my internal motivation. For that, I keep the Ironman where I’ll have to see it every single day. At home, it’s on top of my phone when I wake up in the morning—that’s one time when I can hit the park for a few pull-ups or knock out 15 minutes of pushups in the kitchen. If I miss that, it has to go in my work bag, so that when I drop my laptop in, I see it and make a decision whether or not to hit the gym on the way home. When I travel, it’s on the hotel nightstand.

Reader, let me tell you how much sweeter it feels when you know it was your willpower, not a notification, that got you to move around. I’ve done midnight sprints in a hotel gym, 6 a.m. jump rope on the sidewalk, 6 p.m. squats in the gym. There are times where I go too hard or too light, but I’m learning to listen to my body—not an app—for cues on when it’s ready, and if I skip a workout for drinks with friends or a movie with my wife, I don’t beat myself up. It’s a little part of my brain, my soul that I haven’t given over to the tech overlords. All I needed to get in shape was a good watch.

#6864. The Transformative Experience of Buying Japanese Denim in Japan

I was never a big jeans guy. After a recent trip to Tokyo, I think I’m changed forever.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

A ponytailed man in head-to-toe denim orbits me a third, then a fourth time in quick succession, like his eyes are twin asteroids and my waist a powerful gravitational force. When he stops, suddenly, he calls out, “Thirty-two!” and asks, “Button or zipper?”

I should’ve expected this sort of wizardry when I entered Momotaro Jeans Aoyama, a Tokyo outlet of the highly esteemed Japanese denim purveyor. Though it’s housed on the second floor of a mall just a three-quarter-mile walk from overstimulating, people-packed Shibuya, this tiny shop feels tranquil, purposeful, reverent. Dark indigo pants are folded and piled neatly in wooden cubbies along the walls; thick jackets hang gently from floating racks. Such a scene differs greatly from my earliest jeans-shopping memory.

In my early teens, on an Easter Sunday trip into New York, my mother shooed me into the Times Square Levi’s to buy my first big-boy pair. I never wore jeans. I attended Catholic school for much of my youth, so most mornings I was stuffed into stiff, ugly khakis. In rebellion against forced formality, I’d sport sweatpants or basketball shorts outside school hours.

Naturally, this appalled my fashionable mother, who couldn’t have lived with herself if she dropped her teenage son off at coed social outings wearing swishy Adidas track pants. But why was her remedy to hit a retail store in the busiest neighborhood in America on a holiday weekend? Trying on fur coats in hell would be comparably pleasant.

We dodged shoppers yanking slim cuts off floor displays and dug through unkempt mounds of unfolded denim until Mom found a few sizes in a few washes and forced me into the back changing room to try ’em all on. Of course, I huffed and puffed. Of course, I whinged and complained. But I left with a pair of medium-wash straight legs that at least wouldn’t look embarrassing on a first date.

In the nearly two decades since, as my interest in menswear has graduated from begrudging to professional, mass-market clothing has plunged in quality but spiked in price. Consumers are faced with a harsh reality when buying new: Either replace your decaying polyester-blend garments every few years or buy pricier, sturdier stuff from reputable manufacturers. I diverted toward the latter path a few years ago when I purchased a $150 heavyweight cotton hoodie from American Giant. (Though that may not seem like much to an Esquire reader, it was quite the jump for a J. Crew Factory sales-rack goblin like me.)

I learned of raw, selvedge denim a year later, when a buddy who’s into menswear sent me a Reddit post depicting a pair of jeans standing up all on their own. This was the beauty of raw Japanese denim, my friend told me, which is manufactured in the spirit of heritage American workwear from a time when durability was paramount. Heavyweight. Premium dye. Selvedge, meaning it’s woven on old-school looms from high-quality cotton. (You can also buy cheaper, significantly less impressive Japanese denim, but we’re talking about the reputable specialists here.) I was intrigued until he mentioned how many hundreds of dollars they cost thanks to import fees and general U.S. financial fuckery. But if I were ever on a trip to Japan at a time when the U.S. dollar was stronger than the yen? Maybe I could swing a pair.

In September, I was invited on a work trip to Tokyo, and finally my chance presented itself. When I landed at Narita International Airport, all I could think about was trying on stiff, uncomfortable jeans—talk about a different mindset from that of teenage me in Times Square.

After a week of tight itineraries, I finally sneaked away on my very last day and made my way past the stanchions lining the perimeter of the Momotaro shop. I read online that some folks wait up to 30 minutes to enter; luckily, there is no queue when I arrive.

A handful of employees stand silently behind a broad counter, one of them the man with the ponytail, who approaches me when he sees me checking out the brand’s #100 Standard Straight jeans in 14.7-ounce denim, which is a measurement of weight per square yard of fabric. Instead of asking for my size, he does the circling bit described earlier. I reply to his follow-up question with “zipper,” and he shows me to a narrow changing room toward the back of the space and asks me to remove my shoes. Japanese etiquette. He arrives with two pairs of the very jeans I was checking out. I try them both on, but they don’t feel quite right.

“Anything thicker?” I ask, trying to overcome the language barrier between us. My Japanese is rudimentary; his English is solid in bursts but not completely fluent.

He returns with a straight-cut pair from Momotaro’s classic line that’s 15.7 ounces, but it has a button fly, which I struggle mightily to close. Alas, there are no zippered pairs of this kind left. Yet, of the three, these fit the best around my waist. I’m puzzled until I look closely at the tags. These classic button flies are a 32, but when I return to the standard zippers, I notice he has accidentally brought me a 31—way, way too tight in the crotch—and a 33, a hair too loose.

His eyes widen apologetically when I point out the error, and he rushes back to the stocks to ensure I get a 32 in my hands. They fit absolutely perfectly. Done and done. He’s relieved.

And just like that, I’m ready to walk out a satisfied customer. This is how I’m used to buying jeans, after all. You try ’em, you like ’em, you buy ’em, and if you get home and they’re not quite what you thought they were, you return ’em. But as I will soon learn, there’s more to it at Momotaro. My guy has me put them back on and step out once more.

“Baggy or not baggy?” he asks of how I’d like my jeans to appear.

In front of a mirror, he squats down and folds the bottoms of the legs up to just about my ankle line and slides a transparent clip over the surface to mark its place. An in-house tailor will hem them for me. I change back into my other clothes, and he explains as best he can how to properly take care of my jeans. I shouldn’t wash them too often, but when I do, I should fasten everything and turn them inside out. Oh, and keep them separate from other clothes, because the indigo will definitely run. That’s part of the fun; the jeans are designed to fade handsomely over time based on how and when I wear them. A personalized pair, if you will. Don’t ever dry them in a machine, though. Take ’em out, smooth the creases, and hang to dry in a cool, dark place.

He hands me a little box with a lifetime-guarantee card and explains that if I’m ever back in Japan and need a repair, they’ve got me covered.

A second employee guides me across the hall to a second storefront, where payment and hemming take place. After I show my passport to receive the 10 percent tax reduction for tourists, I swipe my card. $211. That’s it?! By shopping here, I essentially chop a third off the price listed on the Momotaro U.S. website. I’m told to return in 15 or so minutes once the hemming is done, so I dash to a café downstairs for a matcha over which I reflect on my experience.

Whenever I buy anything over a Benjamin, I immediately get buyer’s remorse. That American Giant hoodie? I spent a good few days lamenting that the sleeves were slightly too long before I talked myself out of a return. And yet, when I pick up my Momotaro jeans once they’re ready—they’re bagged neatly and wrapped in plastic to protect them from the day’s ceaseless rain—I feel beyond cared for. When I finally return home and put them on, I don’t fear that I maybe should have purchased a smaller size in case they stretch—they’re not supposed to—because my jeans fit perfectly. They’re right in proportion with my body, sit higher than I’m used to but quite comfortably, and style well with a graphic tee and light jacket.

Maybe I’m shopping at the wrong stores at home. Maybe I need to raise my price floor to get that kind of service. Or maybe we’ve lost something here in the U.S. that’s now attainable only overseas. Maybe I need to get back on a plane to Tokyo ASAP and hit all the stores I missed—Hinoya, Japan Blue, the Flat Head—then take the shinkansen to Osaka and get myself some really thick boys from Iron Heart, where 25-ounce denim is the gold standard.

Or maybe I should just enjoy the jeans I have now, wear them in nicely over the next decade until they’re washed to sky blue and softer than a lamb’s ear. I can look forward to passing them down to whatever kids I have one day. It’ll save us a trip to the Times Square Levi’s.

#6861. Actually, Robert Pattinson Would Make a Perfect James Bond

My colleagues and editors are sick of hearing me say it, but I believe it with my whole chest: The actor playing James Bond should never be more famous than James Bond. There’s something about Bond—his cultural footprint and the dangerous yet seductive life he leads in his stories—that makes him too big to be a footnote in a great actor’s résumé. James Bond creates leading men; James Bond is not for leading men to create.

So you have to know that it’s not easy for me to say this: Robert Pattinson would be fantastic and exactly the new James Bond we’re looking for.

You’ve probably heard by now that Amazon, which acquired full creative control over all things Bond from longtime owners Eon Productions and the Broccoli family, has been searching for the next 007 in a movie to be directed by Denis Villeneuve.

What was once an arena of speculation has exploded into a circus, with different names being bandied about at once—namely Callum Turner, Josh O’Connor, and Jack Lowden. (There is obviously a specific type of white man they’re going for here.) Just when it seemed as if the new Bond would finally be unveiled, there came a rush of two really big names suddenly in the running. Among them is Pattinson, reported by The Hollywood Reporter on November 6, along with Nicholas Hoult. Pattinson secretly auditioned for the role during a break in filming for The Batman: Part II, which halted filming in October when Matt Reeves attended to a personal matter.

No shade to Hoult or any of the other actors, but Pattinson is now the most interesting candidate despite a whiff of overexposure this year. Between The Drama in the spring, The Odyssey in the summer, Primetime in the fall, and soon Dune: Part Three in the dead of winter, 2026 has been shaping up into a banner year for Pattinson. Not since Twilight has the man been so prolific on our screens.

But even before the year began, Pattinson had long ago outgrown his image of a tween heartthrob. He’s a prodigious talent capable of daring choices and executing on them in a way you can’t ever expect. (Who else would say cock like this?) His popular line from The Odyssey—“Somebody get these beggars out of here!”—didn’t become a meme on its own. Pattinson made beggars sound like a slur, and in the same breath, his nervous blinking and quivering smile cemented Antinous as an unconfident weasel whose puffed-out chest was, in fact, filled with hot air. Unlike previous Batman actors, who often played the Caped Crusader like a knight in matte black armor, Pattinson’s interpretation was/is that of an antisocial weirdo everyone stares at like a freak. Which he is.

Like Batman, Bond has also been a role that people misunderstand, sometimes even by the men who play him. (It is not my biases talking here; I really do think Daniel Craig is the only actor to ever nail the nuances and pathology of the character.) Bond is stylish and can say the right thing at the right time, be it to enemies or lovers. But he’s not a dashing or noble hero. He’s a brute in Brunello Cucinelli. A thug with a $9,000 Omega. He literally kills for a living—and had to kill to get that right—and his attachment to his job is downright destabilizing.

Recall in Skyfall, after Judi Dench’s M orders Moneypenny to shoot, Bond copes with endless drinking, womanizing, and gambling. That might sound like a fun weekend in Vegas, but in the context of Bond, his fallout from discovering his expendability led him to a really, really dark path. It is not normal to drink shots with a scorpion on your hand, nor is it healthy to be up in the morning reaching for booze from behind the bar. Bond might live a life many of us men dream of, but it comes at a severe cost. I don’t know what kind of Bond movie Villeneuve has in mind, but if the creative team is lining up like this, we’re in for something special.

You can’t exactly guesstimate what kind of 007 Pattinson might be based on his previous roles. There is truly little common ground between Batman and Bond. But one thing is for sure: If Pattinson gets the part, we are in for a goddamn show.

#6859. I Guess We’re Going Have to Deal with Pete Hegseth for Another Two Months

The House vote to impeach the Secretary of Talking About War failed yesterday, partially because Democratic members were stuck in traffic and missed it. Great work by all.

Well, as of Tuesday evening, we learned that we will continue to be blessed with Secretary of Talking About War Pete Hegseth and his raging, bubbling hormones for at least two more months. The Republicans in the House of Representatives, undoubtedly exhausted from leaking anonymously what a shtunk Hegseth is, ran and hid when it was time to vote on Representative Thomas Massie’s resolution to impeach Hegseth and get his hands off the American military. From Politico:

Tuesday began with another bravura tantrum from Hegseth, who held a press availability in order to stomp and shout. From The New York Times:

At that point, the Republicans were still flexing their independence.

Oh, Ted, you sweet, innocent child. It’s been a decade since the president insulted your wife and you still think he hasn’t stolen the conscience of your party.

Let us also commend the 11 Democratic members who got stuck in traffic and missed the vote entirely. Very strong work there, gang. And those people longing for the leadership of Nancy Pelosi should note that one of the 11 Democrats who missed the vote was ... Nancy Pelosi. This was a banjaxed moment comparable to Mitch McConnell’s decision to let the Democrats haul all the water on Impeachment II back in 2021.

Putative Speaker-elect Hakeem Jeffries said that Tuesday’s vote is “just the beginning.” However, if it were up to me, I would give up on trying to impeach Hegseth on the charges relating to the launching of the war in Iran. Congressional war powers? That horse left the barn halfway through the past century. I’d concentrate on his dreadful mismanagement of the Pentagon, particularly his purge of minority and female flag officers. Still, this was a body blow to Jeffries’s prospects. “Go play in traffic” is not a viable political axiom.

#6858. Nicole Kidman’s Long ‘Almond Cream’ Nails Are the Sophisticated Go-With-Everything Trend to Try Now: Get the Look

This manicure proves long almond nails never go out of style.

Every item on this page was chosen by an ELLE editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

There are certain manicures that never go out of style, regardless of the season. Case in point: Nicole Kidman’s long ‘almond cream’ nails. Spotted at the LACMA Art+Film Gala wearing softly tapered almond nails in a glossy milky shade, the actor’s manicure served as a reminder that winter manicures needn’t be dark or glittery to make an impact.

“Kidman’s nails are a great example of how to wear a longer length without them looking too dramatic,” says Susan Carroll, founder of Young LDN. “The almond shape is one of my favorites because it’s incredibly flattering on the hands. The sides taper in slightly before finishing in a soft, rounded point, which helps elongate the fingers and gives a much more delicate appearance than a square or coffin shape.”

Serving as an ideal entry point for anyone looking to experiment with longer nails without committing to a dramatic stiletto shape, the almond silhouette offers a more wearable alternative. Carroll also recommends adapting the length to suit your lifestyle and comfort level. The appeal lies in its versatility, as it can be easily achieved without the need for extensions. “The shape does a lot of the work, and keeping the color subtle makes the overall look feel much more elegant,” she adds.

Re-creating the look is very straightforward, as the key lies in the shape. Ask your nail technician for a soft almond silhouette with rounded tips, rather than anything too sharply pointed. “The key is making sure the nails are evenly shaped and the cuticles are tidy, as there’s nowhere to hide imperfections with such a simple manicure,” says Carroll. To finish, simply apply two coats of a sheer milky beige polish before sealing with a glossy top coat for a light-reflecting finish.

The result is an effortlessly elegant manicure that feels equal parts timeless and wearable.

#6857. Moda Operandi Marked the Launch of GucciCore Over Cocktails at Oriana

“That couch over there is quite long, and a long couch always leads to long conversations,” Alex Consani whispered, gesturing to the banquette lining Oriana’s amber walls. The supermodel was correct, as guests gathered there en masse to chat all night long. The topic on everyone’s lips? GucciCore, which debuted in Times Square earlier this year, has landed on Moda Operandi.

To celebrate the Cruise collection’s launch and upcoming trunk show on the luxury e-tailer, the occasion called for a glitzy bash at the newish Nolita haunt. It was an evening for close friends of each brand, including runway regular Consani, who arrived promptly at the 7 p.m. start time. “It’s going to be such a fabulous event—Anok Yai might even walk in the building,” she anticipated, adjusting the strap of her vermilion one-shoulder Gucci gown. Her friend Memphis chimed in, “I’m just excited to see what tonight has in store. To be honest, the invite was kind of a mystery.”

Gucci’s creative director Demna has a fondness for mystery and enigma—and guests exuded that elusive after-dark glamour as they began to populate the room. Yai did indeed show up, cloaked in a voluminous shearling Gucci coat. Nicky Hilton slinked past her, donning a sharply cut bomber jacket from the new drop. Lauren Santo Domingo, the mastermind behind Moda, posed with poise in the collection’s cinched blazer. And the likes of Tina Leung and Sabine Getty, who had walked the runway in Milan several weeks ago for Demna’s Spring 2027 outing, also stopped by.

“Demna’s Gucci gives you permission to be every kind of woman. One night you’re the countess, the next you’re the bad girl, and by Friday, you’re the downtown party girl—all in the same week,” Santo Domingo told Vogue. “Being here tonight almost makes me believe I could wear the same dress as Alex Consani. Bringing everyone together at Oriana felt like the perfect New York way to celebrate the GucciCore collection. Fashion should be fun, and so should the parties.”

Moda has found success in curating wearable staples offset by an unexpected twist—and GucciCore is emblematic of that. From softly structured peacoats to tailored suits and the standout new Gucci Starlet crossbody bag, the core capsule wardrobe is downtown-meets-uptown cool.

By the end of the night, “iconic” had become the word of the hour, and Consani, naturally, had the last word on it: “The most iconic thing that you can do is have a good time.”

Lauren Santo Domingo, Cait Bailey Carbone
